PRICE ONE PENNY

GRAPHIC ACCOUNT OF A VISIT TO A

NIANCHESTER PALACE OF INDUSTRY.

The Pressis the most powerful lever of modern civilisation. the pulsations of thought, words, and actions cabled each I day, flashed from every quarter of the globe, are conveyed to ' the human brain by means of the Press. This constitutes the Press the most interesting the most instructive, and the most fascinating invention and production of the ne.,ch of In- tellect. Kings. Emperors, and Governments have been swept from power by means of the Press. Both geod and evil owe much to the influence of the Press. The newspaper forms the chief source of delight, of Interest, and of instruction at 1 the m -wning breakfast-table. If the newspaper suddenly ceased to exist, then half the enjoyment of the world would cease also. Even the advertisement columns form a source of pleasure to many readers. The ladies carefully examine their Society papers for fashions; sales of milinery and dress- goods have special ieterest for the ladies. It was whilst care- fully noting the heads of different advertisements that 1 was attracted by the unique anuouncement of a Gigantie Fur- nishing Establishment at GAYTHORN, Manchester. I determined, after consultation with my wife, e) proceed. to" 7be Pantheon." ct Gaythorn, in order to test the accu- racy of this advertisement. Accordingly we set out, and eventually reached Piccadilly. We made straight for the, "Royal Hotel," at the corner of Mosley-street, and after in- quiry we then took a ear bound for Alexandra Park, which' we were informed. passed the very door of "The Pantheon' at Gaythorn, which is close to the Central Station. We recpsested the guard to stop the car at " Worsley's." On arriving there we found that " The Pantheon "wan a palatial structure, being nothing less than an Immense Warehouse, with huge plate-gless windows forming a frontage over 100 feet. We had ,lo..rdly pti nce to examine the wiedows, so grea4 was our anxiety to exam i • the interior. An icipation Is often more enjoyable than re.. isation, but in this case rea- lisation altogether overwhelmed our anticipation. Our visit to " The Pantheon" proved to be most enjoyable. Upon examining the windows our attention was riveted upon a magnifieent display of Clippendale Furniture, beautifully carved in

BAS-RELIEF. It is the Finest Exhibition of Furxiture of this cllss that I have ever seen. The Cabinets are original in design, and from what we learned afterwards are the very latest— specially drawn and manufactured for Worsley's. I also xotioed some very high class Pollard Oak Furniture for the dining-room, magnificent specimens of bedroom furniture, besides a miscellaneous collection of household requirements. There were many other specimens of furniture on show; but we shall now proceed inside the premises and attempt to give a brief description of what we saw there. Upon passing through the entrance we were immediately struck with amazement by tn.

GORGEOUS DISPLAY. The ground floor of this establishment we were in- formed, had just been re-modelled and re-furnished. My wife immediately expressed astonishment and admiration at the very unique display of the most beautiful and exquiite furnitnre we have ever seen. Magnificent specimen, of bed- room fu niture of the very highest class, at 75, 85, 100, and 150 guineas. We saw the most perfect, the most charming, the most delightful bedroom suites in Chippendale mahogany that it has ever been our pleasure to inspect. Prices rule from the very lowest for Chippendale furniture from 10 to 60 guineas. We have paid visits to the best establishments in and around Manchester, but certainly no establishment in my experiences has such a collection of high-class furnitnae as Worsley's have. We. therefore, ooncluded that we were in an establishment for the rich only, but we soon disabused our minds of that idea upon inspecting the tremendous stock which Is here displayed throughout the various gigantic showrooms. Worsley's cater for the working man, the tradesman, and the merchant with the long purse. Their stock is so immense that you can buy a bedroom suite for £3 155., or you can pur. chase one for 180 guineas. Continuing our inspection of the ground floor, I must mention that the place is beautifully lighted by electricity. You can, therefore, see everything distinctly. The powerful light would soon lay bare any defects, if any existed. Specimen suites of Dining-room Furniiure in pollard oak, specimen suites of Dining-room Furniture in Chippendale mahogany of the most delightful design were marked in plain figures at extremely low prices. Our next attraction was the Carpet Showroom upon the (AMP floor. The display did credit to the gentleman who is in charge of this 'department, and was remarkable in some Instances for the delicate and artistic prlsm4ic effects, while in other cases they present a gorgeous embellishment of delightful hue and brilliance. I noticed that the best productions of the newest patterns for next spring have actually been secured already, and we were given to understand that the next spring's patterns have not yet been shown to any compltitore in the ordin- ary way. We noticed the following prices • Five-frame Brussels,2s aid per yard; special Brussels at 28 4kcl per yard; extra Cut line Brussels, 2s 7id per yard ; superior Brussels at 2s 11Id per yard ; best Five-frame PrllfiSelti at 3s 6d per yard: British-made Axminsters at 3s 3d and 3s 6d per yard: best Royal Axrninsters at 3s 11frd per yard (we have noticed these at 5s 1101 retail in other establishments) ; beat all- Wool Aristos at 5s 6d per yard; the Crompton Axminster at Is lid per yard. The Crompton Axminster is specially recommended as the finest carpet extant, the most beautiful designs, and the loveliest colours procurable. The Princess Seamless Axminster Squares, 12ft by 9ft, at £4 198 9,1. We afterwards took the trouble of comparing the prices as shewn In this department with the prices on other carpet displays In Manchester and district, and the result of our investiga- tions proved to us conclusively that Worsley's carpets as quite 30 per cent below the ordinary retail prices. Worsley's oric-es aro wholesale prices.

wE PROCEEDED UPSTAIRS. The first sYowroom is the Dining-room Furniture Showroom. Sideboards are placed in corridors, with the suites in the centre. We were astonished at the immense variety and the tremendous stock of furniture. Sideboards. with plate-glass backs, are here exhibited at 355., £2 ss, 3 guineas, and 4 guineas; the most extraordinary value, in walnut, mahogany,and oak. Magnificent productions in pol- lard oak at 30 guineas, 35 guineas, 40 guineas. and 60 guineas will show at once the imm-nse variety of the stock. Dining- r ,om Suites, of whicn there are over 150 on show, start from £4155., and go on step by step up to 75 guineas; they are up e olstered in all kinds of materials—velvets, p-gamoid,aud. meroccos Overmantels to match the Sideboaris are also on show. Pining TaLles from 255. to £25. We have paid visits to many furnishing establishments, but the prices fixeu to cull article at Woraeley's simply dety.bewilder,and pulverise any possible competition. We understand every Suite is guar- anteed,and the money will be returned if any reasonable ob- jection be found with them after delivery. worsley's eschew the Jerry furniture makers,and will only trade with manufactures who will guarantee them against loss if it is to be found faulty. We were next conducted to the new Piano Saloon, which is one of the latest additions to " The Pantheon," and has bean reconstructed during the month of October. It has been doubled in size, and forms one of the most attractive features of" The Pantheon." Here we found a magnificent collection of Musical lnstrnments, both new and ,:econd- hand, by all the best makers, at wonderfully low prices. Second-hand Pianos to suit the working class from the very low price of 30s, £2 1 5,.£3 10s, £5, up to £lO. We next saw a colledion of a better doss of seeonci-hand. Pianos ranging from £lO to £2O ; any quantity to select 'from. From this point we now inspected a most wonderful collection of new instruments, all marked at wholesale prices. We were con- vinced upon examination that these instruments are unite 25 per cent less than the retail mnsic-seller's .price Primes by Kirkman, Neumeyer, Bechstein. finpkiuson, may te pur- cnased for 28 guineas. We should strongly advise anyone requiring a good pisnofore to visit Worsley's before pur- chasing.

DAZZLING A PPEARANCE. The Bedstead Showroom at Woreley's presents an ap- pearance of a sea of nt gold. Magnifice specimens of the brassworkera' art are here displayed to perfection. Brass Bed- steeds may be urchased from Zse to £35.. Brass and Iron Bedsteads from 6s St. to 50s. Of course WorsleY's cater for all cl.sses in this department, just as we have described in the other departments. There is the common Iron Bedstead for half a guinea. There is the magnificent and latest pro- duct! n of Hoskins and Sewell for 40 guineas. A Persian ,Bed- stead. enamelled all brass, complete with beaatlful set of drapery and Shetland wool overlay, can be se,ured for a 1784 d. Tiv.l visitor is struck by what appears to be miles of corridors of Bedroom Suites. Tut; wardrobes of each suite stand shoulder to shoulder like a

REGIMENT OF SOLDIERS. Access to each of the Suites is quite a simple matter, sufficient spece being allowed in corridors for examination and inspection. And then she saw a head appear above the top of the wall, and the head was followed by the figure of a man.

She strained her gaze, bat could not discern the features of the strange intruder. The man lingered on the top of the wall a moment, appearing to listen, and then he cautiously descended, by way of the vines, to the ground. Then he halted in the garden, close in the shadow of the high wall. Dora's heart seemed in her throat.

"It can't be Warner," she thought, trembling. "The man cannot be a burglar. If he were a friend to the Narrs, he would ring at the gate. He has come to see me 1"

The man silently quitted the shadow of the wall, and came nearer, studying the features of the house.

Dora had no light, and from his position none was visible in the dwelling. The cottage, indeed, seemed silent and deserted.

The intruder suddenly marked the open win- dow, and stole closer and closer, halting in the shadow of the tree before it.

For the first time Dora saw him fully. With difficulty she restrained the wild cry of joy that arose to her lips. The intruder was the young Squire Weir. Dora leaned out of the window, calling softly and impulsively: " Noel I 0, Noel 1" "Dora 1" exclaimed a quick, glad voice. "I have found you, then? Heaven be praised "How was it possible for you to trace me here, Noel ?" cried Dora, thrilling with her great joy. "I feared you had gone home. I feared you had left me to these people, not daring to contest their claim on me. Oh, I have been so lonely, so miser- able, Noel !"

"-My poor Dora," said the young squire, ten- derly and pityingly. "And you thought I could desert you? I have searched for you everywhere, and only yesterday thought it possible that the Narrs might have taken you out of town. I went to all the railway stations, and at the Waterloo got trace of you, by great good fortune. And here I ao, Dora."

"1 might have known you would not desert me, Noel. It is wonderful how you found me. lam so glad I" "And so am I. Where are the Narrs !" "In the back basement."

"Good'! I have no keys, but it will go hard if I do not find some way to rescue you. First of all, however, I must get nearer to you" "But how ?" questioned Dora, I.nxiously. The young squire cast a quick glance around him. The tree, one great branch of which stretched like an arm before Dora's window, caught his gaze. He scanned it, measured distances with his eyes, and then moved toward it.

no -is come • anThe wants to see you. Will go down to the parlour, or will you see him here? As she asked the question she looked around the neat chamber, as if to examine into its fitness for a reception-room. _

-- • "1 am conning up to you, Dora," he said." Keep watch for us both."

He commenced to climb the tree, soon gaining a foothold among the thick branches. In a few Minutes more he was upon the branch by her window, and so close to hcr that Dora could see the lustre of his eyes and hear his quick breath- ing. She held out her hand to him. _ . _

Tfie bed was set in an alcove, and hidden by curtains of white dimity. The room itself had always served as a lady's private sitting-roorn, and was well adapted for the purpose. "1 decline tosee him at all," said Dora, spiritedly. "I dislike Mr. Warner, and I have no wish to hear any more insults from his lips. If you are my mother—as you claim—you will irotect me from the foul presence of this man." Mrs. Narr frowned.

lie took it and pressed it to his lips. "1-low pale you are !" he said, anxiously. "The Narrs have not iil-treated.you, Dora ?" "Not particularly, but I have been kept shut up here till tills arternooa. The door is locked now. They do not fear that I can escape; and in- deed it would be a difficult undertaking for me alone !" "Has—has Warner been here yet 7" questioned the young squire.

"This is pretty talk to your mother I" she ejacu- lated. "You are my daughter, you are a minor, and consequently obliged by law to obey me; and I command you to receive Mr. Warner as my friend, if not as your own. Things have come to a fine pass, I think, when daughters, if they have been adopted out by a fine family that turned 'en: out poor and helpless, attempt to dictate to their mothers. You have got to see Mr. Warner or you'll regret it—that's all 1" Dora's cheeks flushed with indignation. Yet she controlled herself admirably. "I am in your power, "Mrs. Narr," she said, coldly. "Since I must see your friend,' I will see him here.'

"No; but he is coming, I judge, trom what Mrs. Narr says. She expected him last night, and has been looking for him all day. It was at his ling- gestion this house was taken." Noel's face darkened. His broad brows con- tracted, and he compressed his lips firmly. "And you are in the power of auch wretches as these ?" he cried, indignantly. "Dora, you must leave this pestilential how* this very night. You will not hesitate t.) go with me ?" "Hesitate r cried Dora, in surprise. "How could Have you not said you will be my brother, NoPl. ? Papa trusted you, and so do I I" She put up both her hands to him with a trust- ful look, adding : " rake me away from here, Noel. If I stay here longer, thus threatened, I shall die." Dora retreated again to the window. A minute later, the door again opened, and Felix Warner came slowly and hesitatingly into the chamber.

Dora regarded him in surprise. All his smoothness and assurance seemed gone. He was pale and dejected, and appeared to be in deep trouble. His eyes drooped before her bright glances, and his manner was at once anxious, humble, and deprecating. His new character, however, sat well upon him. His deep melancholy, as perhaps he knew, was becoming to him. _ _

He ha li left Saltair on the previous day, as has been said, and had slept in London the previous night. This day, at the close of which he had now called upon Dora, had been spent by him in the vicinity of the Surrey farm, of which Jack Narr had formerly been sub-tenant, and in a close investigation of the affairs of the Naffs. He had made various discoveries, and now, when be stood before the young girl the picture of sorrow and humility, he was at heart glad and exultant. He paused near the door, and raising his eyes in seeming timidity, exclaimed in a voice of deepest melancholy.:

But Dora suddenly trembled in alarm. The sound of approaching wheels had caught her hearing. What if it should be Warner ?" she asked, in a frightened voice.

"At this hour? Impossible. Yet you can wait till the cab passes, Dora." The sound became louder and louder, and sud- denly ceased abruptly. The cab had halted at the gate of the Black Cottage. _ _

"Dora—" " Miss Chessom, if you please, sir 1" said Dora, 'with spirit. lie came a step near, and looked at her as in anguished pleading. Dora drew closer to the window. Her radiant brown eyes were like glowing stars. The rose tint in her cheeks flickered like a red flame behind an alabaster shade. Pure and dainty and sweet, with a rare and glorious loveliness, she was as much above Warner as an angel is above a demon. He seemed to feel the distance between them, and made as though he would have knelt to her. "Dora," he said, in a broken voice, "I have come back to crave your forgiveness for that cowardly insult of the other day. I did it in a mowent of madness. Forgive me." And now he actually sank on his knees at her feet, and lifted his pleading face, on which were tears.

"It is Warner I" cried Dora, in a panic, as a loud ring was heard at the gate. "We are too late, Noel. We must wait."

She loosened herself from his grasp, and slipped back into the room.

Noel slid along the branch, and ensconced him- self in the deepest shadows of the tree. A sound was heard of bolts being undone, and Mrs. Narr went out with a lantern, and opened the gate to the new-comer.

As Dora had thought, the intruder was Felix Warner.

THE SUNDERED HEARTS;

OR, A WAR OF THE HOUSEHOLD.

By the Author of "The Double Life," "The Bailiff's Scheme," &c., &a.

CHAPTER XXVIII. LIGHT IN DARKNESS.

It was the third day of Dora's sojourn at the Black Cottage. The hour was about sunset. The young girl was sitting by the window, in her upper chamber, deeply thinking. The sash was pushed up, and the summer breeze stole in, and a few rays of sunlight filtered in between the interstices of the thickly-leaved tree that guarded her windows like a sentinel, and dropped like diamond rays upon her dusky, clustering rings of hair. The door was locked from without, Mrs. Narr having the key in her pocket. Upon the front doorsteps, nearly under the window, Jack Nexr sat smoking, and sending up an occasional glance at Dora's window.

Thus doubly guarded, she was yet thinking of escape.

Since entering the room, on being brought from Loudon, she had not been permitted to leave it. Her meals had been brought to her, and she had been allowed to sit at her open window—the trees effectually screening her from all view of or from the street—but she had had no exercise, no employment for her fingers, nothing to read, nothing to do but to think. And thinking had now become a dreary employment. She had been visited frequently by the Narrs ; and felt that, although Mrs. Narr disliked her, Jack was rather kindly disposed to her, and that both looked upon her as a prize, by which they hoped to gain what to them would be a fortune. She supposed that they intended to obtain this money from Felix Warner, who had now becorhe to her an object of utter aversion and detestation. All of these things had been turned over in her mind again and again, until she had grown tired, and now she sighed .hopelessly, thinking how strangely her life had darkened during the last few weeks.

"I suppose," she said to herself, her dusky eyes glooming and her lip quivering, "that Noel has either failed to find me, or has gone back to Weir Hall. He may think, as the Narrs are—or claim to be—my parents, he can do nothing, and that I must remain in their custody till lam of age. But perhaps he is still searching for me in London. He would never think of running down to Chiswick, and there is no hing to give him a clue to my whereabouts. Oh, if I had only been wise on that fatal morning when I chose between my two lovers I I threw away the real for the false. I thought I loved Warmer when I did not. And although lam not sure that I love Noel now, it would not be hard, I think, to love one so brave and true and honest. I am not the first girl who has thrown away the plain, country gentleman for the fine, false city lover."

Again she sighed, and again her lip quivered piteously. After a little she leaned out of the window, and called sottly : " Mr. Narr Jack Narr took his pipe out of his mouth, and, bending forward, looked up, saying; " Well, what is it, Miss Dora? Have you come to your senses ?" "I am so tired," said Dora, pitifully. "I have never been so long in one room at a time in my life. Just think how hard it is to shut me up in this way, I who was brought up in the country, as free as the birds, and who love the sunshine and the fresh air as I love my life. Let me walk in the garden just one half hour! Narr knocked the ashes off his pipe against the door-stone, in a thoughtful manner, and then said, pointing the pipe over his shoulder signifi- cantly : "She won't let you, Miss Dora. There's no use asking her; still you can try it if you want to."

" %Voce* rou ask her ?" pleaded the young girl. "She 'd refuse me at once."

Nerr reflected a few moments.

Then he looked up, winking -mysteriously, and whispered, shrilly: "I say, Miss Dora, is a walk in the garden worth ten pounds to you? The old woman's mad because I had a drink or so lately, and took all my money last night when I was asleep." Dora comprehended his drift. "I 'll give you ten pounds if you 'll let me out a little while," she answered.

She took her pocket-book from her bosom, and produced a ten-pound note, which she fluttered out of the window, retaining her hold on it. Narr was all eagerness in an instant. His round red face grew yet redder, and he sprang up and came under the window, his eyes sparkling. _

. ~ . "Money first, Miss Dora," he whispered: "Then I 'll ask the old woman." - .. - _- -

Dora dropped the bank-note. It fluttered within Narr's grasp, and he put it in his pocket. Dora restored her pocket-book to its hiding- place, and waited. Narr went back to the door-stone, sat down, and took up his pipe. After a minute or two he called his wife. Mrs. Narr speedily made her appearance from an inner room.

" Well, what is it?" she demanded. "Girl all right ? " "Not by a good sight," replied Jack, with an upward glance. "I jest happened to look at her, and I declare for 't she's getting as pale and peilked as a last year's ghost. I tell you, Mr. Warner won't be so took with her if she 's so thin and ghastly when he comes." Mrs, Narr looked up at the window, and met Dora's gaze. "She don't look quite well," she admitted. "But what can I do ? " "Tr.,. her around the garden half an hour."

Mrs. Narr looked pertUrbed. "How can I? " she asked, in an undertone. "She might call out for help, or Sir Graham Gal. lagher might see her." • "Sir Graham Gallagher's in better business thau a-setting a-top of stone walls a-looking_ for gals as he don't know exists," remarked Jack Nary, more forzibly than grammatically. "Most likely Sir Graham Gallagher's away from home. If he aint, he's settin in his lib'ry a-swigging wine, as I should do in his place. And if he saw the gal, it's easy to tell him she's your darter. You don't seem up to business. Take the gal by the arm and trot her around; and when she gets tired march her up to the room. That's my ad- vice."

_ Mrs. Narr pondered on it, looked up again at the pale, eager face, and said: "You 're right, Jack, for once. She shall have the fresh sir."

She entered the cottage, mounted the stairs, and unlocked Dora's door, entcring the room. "Come, put on your bat," she said. "I am going to take you around the garden." Dora hastily obeyed, and Mrs. Narr marched her down stairs and out of doors, keeping a tight hold upon her arm all the time. The garden was in a sadly neglected condition, and the grass-grown walks led through tangles of shrubbery, all matted and grown together; but, after her long imprisonment, the fresh air, the glorious sunlight, and the sweet odours of the flowers from the adjoining garden were inexpres- sibly delightful to Dora. For half an hour she sauntered about, guarded by Mrs. Narr, and during that time she saw no one save the Narrs, and heard no one besides them, except a gardener pruning his peach trees on the opposite side of the partition wall, and singing as he worked. At last the man mounted a ladder, and his head began to appear above the wall, Mrs. Narr hurried Dora up to her room and locked her in. At about six o'clock the girl's supper was brought up, and she was left alone for the night. The Nacre secured all the doors and retired to the basement kitchen, a quarter of the house that suited them best, and here Narr smoked, while his wile talked of the best plan by which they could secure possession of the money on Dora's person. "I don't know know but the best way would be to go in and take it from her when she 's asleep," the woman muttered. "She 's no business to keep that money from us, and I mean to have it. I 'll think the matter over, and get the money with the least trouble." Meanwhile Dora continued to sit at her open window, and the day faded, and the long summer twilight brooded softly over the scene, tilling the garden with a hundred shadows. The twilight was deepening into night, and Dora was begisuing to think of drowning all her sorrows in sleep, when she beard a. sound that tin i'led her pulses and stied her heart to wilder bea! lugs. It was a sound as of some one scaling the stone wall fronting the lonely street. "Some one is there," thought the girl, all at once eager and impassioned. "Who can it be? Not a friend ? Not Nool?"

She crouched in the shadows, watching and listening.

THE MOTOR CAR TOUR.

RESULTS OF THE RUN TO BRIGHTON.

The following official report of the motor car run to Brighton on Saturday has been issued Brighton, November 16.—1 t was at first decided by the committee that only cars officially tested and duly passed should enter for the Bright-_,n ride. This wae,hew- ever, finally overruled, and it was made an open evect. Instead, therefore, of (We' the 20 efficient cars entering as prearranged, all kinds of experimental machines joined in the demonstration. Owing to the crowd no real start could be made tietil Brixton was reached, when 22 cars only were started, as they arrived at long intervals. Along the reacl,well-known timekeepers were placed. including Messrs. Penn, Coleman, Jack, Dring, and Swindley. The folkwing cars duly arrived at Brighton :—First at 2-33, a Belle() voiturette ; at 2-47, a Bolleee car. Neither of these fol- lowed the instructions to wait at Reigate. Their time record, however, was extraordinary, as they each carried two persons, and were not racing. Our own c it's endeavoured to keep to the original pro- gramme. The pilot car and three British Motor Syndi- cate cars were r•all four in their proper place at Preston Park, Brighton, punctually at 4-30, where they were re. ceived by the Mayor of. Brighton. An American car fol- lowed immediat, ly. The two Bollees had also gone through, and a new Beeston motor cycle, making eight cars in all which were in good time. The next hour brought four more, making twelve. The whole of the rest of the 22 cars arrived in Brighton at intervals dur- ing the evening, with two exceptions. One broke down at Patceams, the valves being choked with mud. The other stepped at Reject°, and tame in during the night. All these motors behaved splendidly, with tee exception of the one car referred to. No accident of any kind hap- pened to our cars. The Crawley accident was caused by the Duryea (Atnerican)car through no fault of the driver One of our members who witnessed. it states that the child darted out from the crowd and ran under the machine. After the banquet on Saturday evening the committee had the satisfaction of examining 20 cars in Messrs. Du Pont's stables, and beyond the lower half of each vehicle being covered with mud, they were in per- fect condition and ready to take the road again imme- diately. Eighteen of these cars were lent to members by the British Motor Syndicate. The committee decided to award gold medals to the first eight motors which arrived in good tine. The fact that 20 motors out of 22 which left Brixton arrived in Brighton during the evening without accident exceeded the committee's most sanguine expectations."

CAPITAL" I AT.

The public appeal issued by a conterenze of Liberal politicians for the Armenian agitation to be taken up by the Libral party on party lines was the main topic of conversation in political circles at the beginning of the week. Ther-, is a general agreement that the scrupulou,,, en(lea v•ur to run a non-party agitation has not met wit., the success which was expected, hut it is felt that the appeal for a change in tactics has come too late for present purposes. The agitation has certainly been mainly a Liberal agitation, but the Liberals organising it have hampered and restrained themselves in order to: secure the co-operation of Conservative sym- pathisers. The response has been disappointingly small, and most of those Conservatives who have come forward have still further restrained them- selves and their allies in order not to embarrass their friends in the Government. The friends of the Armenians feel now that they might have secured much more if they had frankly made use of the mighty engine which an avow@dly political agitation on party lines would have put into their hands. But the opportunity has gone past.

Another aspect of the education question was empha.ised on Monday by the unveiling of a mem- orial table of Joseph Lancaster, the father of the whole modern system of educating people for the sake of education. His famous words are the char- ter of unsectarian schools and of the modern school boards, and at this digs are worth reproducing. He said: "Above all things education ought not to be made subservient to the propagation of the pec- uliar tenets of any sect beyond its own members. It then becomes undue influence, like the strong taking advantage of the weak, and yet reverence for the sacred name of God and the Scriptures of truth, detestation of vice, a love of veracity, a due attention to duties to parents, relations,and society, carefulness to avoid bad company, civility without flattery, and a peaceable demeanour may be incul- cated in any seminary for truth without violating the sanctuary of private religious opinion in any mind." It was to combat this principle of nnsec- tarian education that, eighty-five years ago, the National Society was formed, and the battle then commenced by the clerical party in the English Church is the battle which is still being fought to-day,

Home Rule for Rhodesia is the meaning of the resolutions passed by the white settlers of Salis- bury. It is not a vague idea, but one of a formal and thought-out character, showing that the men who passed them had been brooding silently over the matter for some time. Perhaps they owe it to Mr. Chamberlain's famous despatch in which he launched the proposal of Home Rule for the Rand. A scheme which is good for dissatisfied Johannes- burghers cannot be wrong for disconteated Rhode- sians. The Colonial Office would be left with the Control of the foreign relations of Rhodesia—its relations for example, with the Boer Republic; and there the Home Government's functions would practically end. The Chartered Company is not to be quite kicked out of the country as yet, as it would be left for the present with a majority on the territorial or Home Rule council. The scheme is much like a recent forecast given by Mr. Rhodes himself; if that gentleman had not grievously offended the framers of it by his railway policy, we might have jumped to the conclusion that it had been conceived for the purpose of exalting him to the place of power. At present Rhodesian settlers Can scarcely tell by whom they are governed, or what influences direct the Chartered Company's representative or the High Commissioner, and the Salisbury section of them would cut the Gordian knot by governing themselves.

A large deputation of trades unionists waited up- on the President of the Board of Trade on Tuesday afternoon, to bring before him a large batch of reso- lutions affecting his department which were passed at the recent Trades Union Congress at Edinburgh. They dids not include the sensational demands which made the Norwich Congress famous. The Trades Union Congress has returned to more limited and immudiate issues, and the matters submitted to the Board of Trade were mainly practical and what would be called minor departmental matters. Mr. Ritchie naturally took the opportunity to ntildly warn the trades unionists againsttinviting too much Government interference in matters of trade and industry, but he returned a sympathetic answer to most of the specific requests, especially with regard to the appointment o more railway inspectors from the ranks of railway workers.

If Mr. Chauncey Depew succeeds Mr Bayard as - United States Ambassador at St. James's, the appointment will be a welcome one, both to the British Government and the English people. Mr. Depew knows this country well, and entertains towards it the most friendly feelings. He has mixed largely in the best London society, is a friend of the Prince of Wales, an attractive social speaker, a man of great tact and shrewdness, and will carry on the traditions of friendship between the two Countries which Mr. Bayard has done so much to maintain and confirm. fle would enter upon his duties as a perfectly familiar 'figure to the leading English politicians and the freruenters of the West End drawing-rooms.

The Chancellor of the Exchequer has very thoughtfully laid his hand on the autocar. Every one of these vehicles between one and two tons will pay two guineas and between two and three tons in weight three guineas, in addition to the present rate of carriage duty. Furthermore, it would seem that every self-propelled bicycle and tricrle Nc7:l be liable to a license at the rate of one guinea per annum. For cars over one ton and under three tons a guinea will have to 'be paid unless they are used as public vehicles, when the present htr,knay carriage rate of fifteen shillinvA will be applied. The public " 'bus " of the future will be charged £1 17s. or ..E3 18s., according to weight. The weights will be of the unladen vehicle, and exclusive of accumulators, water, and fuel. The new licenses -will come into force in January.

The letter of Mr. J. M. Maclean 'has had a tre- mendous effect upon Conservatives, both of the old and the new school. He has merely given voice to the opinions and feelings of nearly every Conserva- tive member who sits behind the Ministry in the 'House of Commons. Perhaps they will not all ad- mit it openly, but in Private, among themselves, in the Tory clubs. and in the smoking-room of the House of Commons, the predominance of the Liberal Unionists in the Government has been a subject of bitterest complaint on the lips of every Tory. Mr. Mactean. moreover, has shot his bolt at an awkward moment. A large section of the clergy and their lay friends are on the brink of rebellion against the education policy of the Government, and as soon AS the meagre little measure of the Ministry makes its appearance the mutiny may even take shape. The Conservative section of the Cabinet is blamed only indirectly, as the refusal of the 'Church's demands is attributed to the influence of the Liberal Unionists. Nor is education the only point on which the Liberal Uuioniets have diverted the Conservative pelicy from its natural course. There are several evidences of the same sinister force at work, and regarding some of them the Tories have felt keenly. Mr. Maclean, therebre. has reawakened all the old sense of grievance and wrong, but the strong subjection to discipline will prevent it. .o far as the House of Commons is con- cerned. from .taking any shape except that of angry denunciations by the Church party.

The latest issue of Chartered shares is going off so unsatisfactorily that it is notfunlikely that the directors will have to submit some new under- writing arrangement, to the shareholders. It will be remembered that the underwriting'schetne sub- mitted to the shareholders the other day was received with much disfavour, and it was with- drawn on the understanding that the present shareholders would take up the new issue anJ provide the million agked for on the basis of £2 a -share. It would seem that the shareholders have 'formed a :luster estimate of the value of Chartered stock than the directors. Whether the market operations of a syndicate of uuderwriters will avail to launch the stock remains to be seen.

It is stated that Sirdar Sir Herbert Kitchener was engaged with Lord Salisbury at. Hatfield almost the whole of Sunday. On 114mday he was enter- tained by the Queen, and yesterday he was most completely engazeo at the War Office with Lord Wolseley and other heads of dt-partments. visit to Lord Salisbury is considered most important S n military circles, and officers are looking. forward to another advance in the Soudan early next :'ear.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

Coats and wraps for young girls are all of the loose flowing type, and those intended for the very young children are distinguished by full folds fall- ing from yokes or collars. The paletDt form is, however, much prettier for girls over nine, and the old or womanish coat sleeves is overcome in a delightful manner by cape sleeves.

An excellent little coat for a little girl is made in grey cloth. It hangs in double box-pleats, straight down the front, while at the neck is a round collar, almost a cape, reaching to the shoulders, straight revers in the front fastening back over pointed revers, hemmed with black astrachan. The hat which crowns it is made of felt, trimmed with a couple of quills and bouad with a piece of ribbon. _ _

A thoroughly useful coat and skirt costume and a smart one withal is of black and white striped frieze used in conjunction with facings of bright green velvet, bordered by inch-wide straps of white kid. The coat is fastened by small gold buttons set in groups of three.

Another coat suitable for clothe .of various colourings and qualities is double-breasted, with a straight front and the new setaiepouffed sleeves. The collar is cut medici fashion and lined with fur.

A new dress for the late autumn is of Prussian blue cloth, the skirt bordered with rows of fancy braid. The bodice is similarly trimmed, and in shape is an open jacket with double revers, the upper one forming a cape over the) shoulders, the lower ones turned back from a loose front of ivory lawn, drawn in at the waist under a wide sash of turquoise.

A pretty dress for deep mourning has the bodice made of crape, out-lined with a knotted trimming of black silk braid and two rouleaux of dull black silk. The skirt is moderately and trimmed with crape and braiding, with a high folded belt of the folded silk.

Every Parisienne with a reputation for smartness to maintain includes a short saoque coat in her autumn wardrobe. It is very becoming to slender long-waisted wearers, and is sometimes in their ease transformed into a Russian tunic, and then resembles a very ample blouse, drawn in at the waist under a decorative belt. A deep collar of fur forms a pointed yoke, and a narrow band edges the wrists. _ _

Some handsome gowns are being fashioned by the leading Parisian modistes of liberty velvet. Often these are trimmed with thick black braid. The bell-shaped skirt has a series of flounces, each set on and edged with a narrow line of braid. Sometimes the bodice is loose-fitting and frequently of the bolero order, opening over a vest of ivory silk. Among the favourite shades in this material are green, blue, chestnut, dark brown, and plaid. Some of the prettiest dancing dresses for girls who have not yet passed their first youth are extremely simple in style. An especially charming one of palest green nun's veiling has a well-shaped skirt, short enough to clear the ground nicely, and edged with a thick ruche of chiffon and satin ribbon in exactly the same shade. The full bodice, cut round at the neck, merged in a wide swathed corselet of green satin, and the berthe consisted of a very full ruche of the pale green chiffon, the full Übe sleeves being finished with a similar ruche. White shoes, stockings, and glovese were to be worn with this.

Square-necked hodiees arc again in favour, and very becoming they are, as a rule; while the moderately low round necks are still fashionable. Very low bodices and exposed shoulders are bad form. Theatre and dinner gowns are often only slightly open at the neck in either V or square shape; but the V must always be avoided by people with long, slender necks; it emphasises the length ungracefully. Sailor and reefei costumes in bright red are popular for small girls, and little boys wear the highwayman type of coat in red, with hat of the same colour. For infants' wear, crearn-whitie, trimmed with beaver, mink, or skunk, is chief favourite for outdoor wear, and short-waisted frocks of soft silk or cashmere for the house. The skirts are invariably tucked, and the sleeves fuli and {drooping. -

TII7, 'IAITNER, SATURDAY, NOMFER 21, 1896.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

It would appear as tlicugh the remark made by the -o Duke of Malborough, to the effect that although b.enheun was a very fine memorial of the dead it was a particularly expensive memorial to the living, was not very wi.'e of the mark, for no less than £5,000 has had to be expanded upon the rooms set apart for the Prince and Princess of Wales and Princess Victoria, on t he occa- sion of their visit—Two Consecutive American Duchesses with a plentiful supply of dollars, have done marvels in making the house habitable, but buildings covering seven acres are not easily brought up-to-date. Those of my readers who are not already aware of the fact, may be interested to learn that it was not until sixteen years after the famous victory that Blenheim Palace was finished, though half a million was voted for its erection by the House of Commons. When the Court is at Windsor, the most frequent visi- tars to Her Majesty's aviary are the children of Princess Henryof Battenburg, who rarely miss a day in "coming to feed the birds." The birds in question know the time of the little folks arrival, and are keenly on the alert to avail themselves of the proffered hospitality. Hither also, when on a visit to the Queen, come the children of the Duke of Connaught, who have a special interest in the aviary, independently of the birds, as immediately in front of it live their own pretty little white Canadian guinea pigs of a special breed, with long rough coats, and graceful timid ways. They were unspoiled, and were a present from the Marchioness of Lorne. Her Majesty still frequently drives round the aviary on her daily visits to Frogmore, but rarely alights from the carriage. November, as we most of us know, is the great month for fireworks, not only on the: earth below, but also in the heavens above. The grand display,however, which only occurs three times in a contury,and was sup- posed to take place on the 14th and 15th inst., was not visible, owing to the deplorable state of the weather. The last great meteor shower was on the night between November 13th and 14'h,in 1866. What these metenrs are forms a sort of romance in astronomy. They may be, we are told,either fragments of worlds that have broken up, or they may be superfluous material, flying round and looking for an opening. Apropos of fireworks, the actual cost in the first in- stance, of one of the set pieces which form so magni- ficent a part of the display at the Crystal Palace, near London, during the season, amounts to no less than £350. Subsequent reproductions cost about LSO each. A fact too, in connectiGn with these vast firework pic- tures is,that occasionally the original design is furnished by an artist of some standing. When there is nothing startlingly new to change the face of fashion ,small details are made points of as up-to- date marks and the fashionable woman is just now pay- ing special attention to ruffles destined to adorn her neck and wrists. These said ruffles are mostly built of pleated 'chiffon, mounted on to a soft ribbon band and can easily be tacked into the bodice. Sometimes the chiffon is scalloped, and edged with lace, but although decidedly more artistic, it is somewhat expensive wear. Long gossamer sleeves for evening gowns are now in high favour,and the mode will be a welcome one to those of us possessed a thin or otherwise unlovely arms. They are made either of fine lace over Base, or entirely of lisse, and the amount of labour bestowed upon them is fre- quently startling. Only the other night, when dining out,l espied one particular pair boasting no less than twenty-four horizontal tucks in less than two inches. Grebe has come back into favour again, and it cer- tainly has a good deal to recommend it,for it is not only inexpensive but very effective. Moreover, no amount of rain will spoil Wand this is a consideration in these Isles of ours. Tippets with long stole ends are made for it,but grebe is really prettier built in the form of a short boa.

There seems alas, very little doubt that intemperance among all grades of our sex is on the increase, and that the propensity is increased by the facility with which wine and spirits can be procured of licensed grocers and confectioners is only tco apparent, for it is no uncom- mon thing to see women dropping into these latter es- tablishments with clnsiderable frequency, during their walks abroad, ostensibly for a biscuit which is usually purchased for appearance sake, in reality for the desired stimulant. In our social life, less wine is apparently drunk than was the case a few years back, but a great deal more liqueur, which figures at luncheon, dinner or supper parties is indulged in. It is gravely announced thatupon the recommendation of the Comptroller of Prison Industries, female convicts are henceforth to be employed in dressing dolls. It is easy to imagine the maddening impatience which the monotony of oakum-picking must provoke, and it is pleasant to think that it is to be wholly abolished as a task for women prisoners. It is added that the extraor- dinary substitute of putting them to dress dolls, is only an experiment and doubtless many of the productions will be more comical than the notion which has occurred to the ingenionsComptroller. It has always been under- stood, that the difficulties in the way of useful and pro- fitable prison labour bsve been its cempetition with the ordinary industries and why this does not apply to dressed dolls, is not very obvious. If the women are to dress them, there is no reason why the men should not make them, in the prisons, the more so because very nearly all the dolls are" made in Germany," and their manufacture would not interfere with home industries. There still remains the larger question, what is to become of all the prison dressed dolls ? Upon this point the enterprising Comproller does not enlighten us. In many houses where a few years ago, men servants were deemed essentially necessary, parlourmaids are now taking the place of first, second and third footmen, and this has greatly raised the wages of women ser- vants, as well as enlarged their sphere of usefulness. With regard to their dress, in some mansions a species of livery is imperative, but when this is not the case the gown must be black, of the simplest style possible, and the whole appearance severely neat. Long flowing ends to caps are unheard of, the bead covering must he close and small, and the bibbed apron wide and long in the skirt.

The danger whichlurks in perfumed flowers, though it has been recoonised to a certain extent, has never been paid safilcient attention to. Medical men, as a rule, encourage the presence of flowers in sick rooms object- ing only to strong-scented ones. But. It is never really wise to place cut flowers about a sick room at all, for they throw off carbonic acid gas, as they decay, and be- sides. and more important than that, they exhale vola- tile oils of a poisonous character. When there are many flowers, it is very much as if a saucerful of the poison-. one 611 were placed in the room to evaporate. Here are a few rules, which according to a German doctor we must Carefully fellow, if we would enjoy good health. We must eat fruit for breakfast and luncheo,, ; avoid pastry, shun muffins, crumpets and buttered toast, eat wholemeal bread, decline potatoes if they are served more than once a day, drink no tea or coffee, walk four miles a day, take a bath every day, wash the face every night in hot water, and sleep eight hours a night! Every housewife has more or less experienced the diffi- culty of getting her raisins stoned for the Christmas puddings and cakes, but this worry (and the mysterious disappearance of a considerable portion of the fruit,may henceforth be awarded, as stoned raisins are now obtain- able of most grocers. The novelty hails from America, and cleanliness is insured by the use of patent steam machinery. " MADGE."

INTERESTING MARRIAGE AT SOUTH- PORT. GRIFFITH-CALVERT.

At Derby Grove Primitive Methodist Chapel, Southport, on Thursday afternoon, a marriage of local interest took place, when Mr. John Griffiths, of Church-street, Warrington, and Miss Annie Calvert, daughter of the Rev. J. Calvert, formerly Primitive Methodist minister of Warrington, were united in the bonds of matrimony. The father of the bride, assisted by the Rev. Charles Finch,of Warrington,and the Rev. G. Taylor,of Southpert,performed the cere- mony. The bride was dressed in *hire Japanese silk, trimmed with pearls and Brussels lace, and wore a large hat, trimmed with plumes and silk. The bridesmaids were Miss Alice Griffiths, Miss Emily Spencer, Miss Lily Dale and Miss Emmeline Griffiths. After the wedding ceremony the party adjourned to the house of the bride's father and the wedding breakfast was partaken of. The newly-married couple departed for Wales where the honeymoon is being spent. They were the recipi- ents of many handsome piesews including a set of carvers, silver breakfast, cruet, silver bread fork and ornamentd vases from the employees of Messrs. J. Griffiths avid Company, general warehousemen at Latchford and Warrington. The bridegroom's present to the bride was an opal diamond gold brooch ; from the bride to the bridegroom, a gold seal; and from the bridegroom to the bridesmaids gold bangleA and gold necklets,

AROUND THE MEDITER-

RANEAN.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
BY DoiA M. JONES.

IV.-GRANADA.

SS'. Midnight Sun.

Perhaps one ought to go through the torture of heat, drought, and mosquitces, in Seville and Cordova. to ap- preciate the full delight of waking in Granada. The dis- tinct coolness of the air after the plains is in itself a delight, and then in the silence of the early morning you can hear the musical tinkle of water from innumerable little springs. You open your door and came out on a courtyard, with birds and fountains and roses and flower- ing oleanders,ancl you appreciate the wisdom of the old Moorish monarchs in pitching their slimmer camp in this delightful retreat,'the springs of which at fed from the snows of Sierra Nevada.

On going a little way to the right, along the road as you come out of the door of the Hotel Washington Irv- ine, roe catch sight of the Alhambra, the Red Castle of the Moors, built of reddish sandstone, and clinging like an eagle's eyrie to the edge of the cliff that frowns down upon the ravine of the Durro. The crumbling walls of the historic fortress are overgrown with vivid green creepers, and even as we saw it at the end of the long drought it was a place of gardens and streams. A bold red hill partly planted with olives rose behind it, and out of a grove of green peeped the white walls of the Generalife, or sumtrer place of the khalifs. Behind and beyond rose the long serrated range of the mighty Sierra Nevada. The hill on which the Alhambra stands is planted with English elms, sent over by the Duke of Wellington, whose descendants still possess the tract of land in this neighbourhood bestowed on Wellington by the gratitude of the King of Spain at the close of daft Peninsular War. Find your way to the edge of the hill, and the whole city lies beneath you, a mass of roofs and domes and towers, and white walls gleaming in the morning light. Tne Cathedral, built on the site of the Grand Mosque after the con- quest of Granada by Ferdinand and Isabella the Catho- lic,is easily to be discerned. The part of the town which surrounds it is called Santa AI, and marks the site of the Spanish camp during the siege. A fire broke out in the camp,and the tents of the Christian soldiery were destroyed. There was a moment's yielding to panic,and a talk of withdrawal; but the queenly cour- age and Christian faith of Isabella soon raised the droop- ing spirit of the army. "Your wooden huts have been burnt," she said, " build them of stone, as a sign that this ground on which we stand shall never be yielded again to the infidel." And so Boabdil the Moor and his garrison had to watch,day by day,thia new city growing on the plain,and their hearts Rank within then a ; for the glory of the Moorish rule had departed, and they had only an effete tyranny to oppose to the fiery chivalry of Aragon and Castile. The modern city of Granada is al- most entirely Christian. The old Moorish town lay in the ravine of the Durro, on the opposite side from the Alhambra Hill and very near the present gipsy encamp- ment.

We were snore annoyed by beggars in Granada than we had previously been in Spain. One determined old person se.th a horribly deformed hand and arm, pestered us exceedingly in the Cathedral. but when we took our carriages to drive to the Carthusian convent, we fondly imagined ourselves rid of him. Nothing of the sort; he took advantage of his knowledge of the neighbourhood to come round a short cut and when WP alighted at the door there we found him,waving his withered limb like a banner, and repeating his tale of woe with untired persistence. In driving through the streets about the Cathedral we were struck, as in other parts of Soain, with the heipht and handsome appearance of the houses; hut the im- pression made by the suburbs between the Cartuja and the gipsy quarter was very different. The stucco•fronted houses had an indescribable air of dilapidation. The white wall spaces, were relieved with iron balconies, often filled with flowers, heavy wooden shutters, blinds of every hue, and strings of scarlet capsicums hanging out to dry. Many of the houses were built with loggie at the top. Donkeys and mules laden with panniers,and the driver perched precariously on the top of the load, filled the streets, while now And again we met a string of muzzled goats coming down from the mountains. In the dusty. rubbisti_trevm oprn spaces where two or three roads met, there would usually be a man sitting on the ground under a rough canvas shelter among his figs and melons, and here and there we came upon a church with a meeting-house front and a rude belfry, crudely painted in violet and green. The Generalife Gardens extend high up the Alhambra hill. The house itself is a plain-looking white erection with a completions tower ; and, like other Moorish buildings, its exterior gives one no idea of the lavish decoration within. Its chief attraction consists in the courts, enclosed with high box hedges and adorned with fish-ponds and fountains, where the khalif used to retire with his ladies when he wished to relax the bonds of state. One particularly fine 'vpress is railed the Sul- tana. It seemed strange so late in the year to see Eng- lish roses growing ananng the myrtles and oleanders of these Eastern bowers ; hut not so strange as the thought that these crumbling palaces, with all that their name recalls of heroism and splendour, should come to be a mere stage in a tourist's progress, a holiday marvel for wanderers from an alien land.

THE GRAIN TRAFFIC.

The Salford Corporation is says the " Mancheste Guardian," to be cotwratulated upon its foresight in deciding to build grain warehouses at the docks. These warehouses will directly benefit the borough in various ways, by helping to cheapen the people's food, tiv pre- paring the way for the establishment of the flour-milling industry en a large scale in the neighbourhood of the docks, and ultimately, no doubt, by ”eturnina a hand- some profit on the capital invested. Indirectly, though not less substantially, the propose grain warehouses will benefit Salford by helping the Ship CAnal, and the value to the Canal Company of proper appliances for dealing with grain cargoes may he inferred from the remarks of the Chairman at nearly every half-yearly meeting of shareholders since the opening of the water- way. It is not ton much to Par that the diversion of an important shsre of the grain traffic which now finds its wis. • into this district through Liverpool. Fleetwood, and Hu!! is ibmlutely essential to tt-e succ,-ss of the Canal L re Ti7orpool alone imported about two million A (.dlv• 20 000 ton, or 1 per cent of that anurstit, CAMP up the Ship Canal. This rear great pro- eress has been made in diverting shipments to the Salford docks, and between Jaouary and Italy 25.000 tons of grain were imported direct by a single firm. Since the opening of the cotton season in September the shipments of maize, wheat, anA oats from America have increased rapidly. About 8.750 tons were landed at Salford in October and over 8 000 tons during the first half of the current e,nonth. whilst something like 15,000 tons more is now afloat for Manchester in steamers from New York and Southern ports. All this is, however, as a mere drop in the ocean when comnared with the im- portatisn through other ports, and leaves room, there fore, for enormous exnansion. That this expansion is bound to take place is an economic e,or Vary to the proposition, already demonstrated, that grain can he shipped from America to Manchester, or Salford, by the Ship Canal for 54. 71-1. per ton less than by Liverpool. and to the surrounding towns at a considerable though somewhat smaller saving To deal with the existing traffic the accommodation at the disposal of the dock authorities is already severely taxed, and but for the timely action of Salford this important branch of the trade of the port would very soon have been seriously hampered in its deyelopment.

THE PARAFFIN LAMP OUTRAGE AT WARRINGTON.

HEAVY SENTENCE AT LIVERPOOL ASSIZES.

At the Liverpool Assizes, on Monday, before hdr. Justice Bruce, a young man named John Hares, a labourer, living at 2, Berry's-yard, off Tanners'-lane,was indicted for having at Warrington, en the 4th August, maliciously wounded his wife by throwing a lighted lamp at her. It may be remembered that on the day lin question, according to the evidence given at the Warrington Police Court,the prisoner commenced drink- ing at an early hour. For the previous six weeks he and his wife had been living with the mother of the latter. Mrs. Hares remonstrated with her husband several times during the day, as he was not only on the spree, but I was pawning articles of clothing out of the house. The result was that the couple were quarrelling during the greater part of the day, and in the evening Mrs. Hares weut into a neighbour's house. At 8.30 the prisoner entered his mother-in-law's house and asked for his wife, but not finding her commenced to quarrel with the old lady. The complainant, who was listening to what was taking place,became elarmed for the safety of her mother, and rushed in the house for her protection. Tbe prisoner became infuriated, and picked up an ashpan to throw at his wife, but it was taken from him. He also picked up the fender, but that was taken from him •, but lastly he seized hold of a lighted y araffin lamp which stood on the mantel-piece, and, remarking that he would have an execution in his house,hurled it across the narrow apart- ment. It struck theswife on the head and knocked her down. The lamp exploded, and the woman's clothes were immediately in flames, but they were extinguished as quickly as possible, the prisoner himself throwing:his jacket round his wife. In terrible suffering she was re- moved to the Infirmary. The prisoner was at once ap- prehended and charged with the offence, and his reply was "I wish it had gene through her heart,l wish it had killed her." For some time her life was in great danger. Prisoner said he was drunk at the time of the assault, and he did not know what he was doing. The jury fcund the prisoner guilty, and the Judge, commenting upon the prevailing ',practice among a cer- tain class of the community of throwing lighted paraffin lamps as missiles, sentenced prisoner to three years' penal servitude.

"AS MANY LIVES AS A OAT."

ADVENTURES OF A FIREMA.N.

When a member of the staff (says the " Newcastle Daily News ") went in search of Mr. John Smith,a stoker on board the s.s. " Polo," trading between Hull and Newcastle-on-Tyne, he found that interesting personage busily engaged in the engine room of his vessel, t that moment moored alongside the Newcastle Quay. Mr. Smith was on board the ill-fated steamer "Betty "when she collided with a Hartlepool vessel and SUNK IN FIFTEEN MINUTES, the crew escaping by means of their own boats. This was sufficiently interesting for a chat, but the real object of the visit was to ascertain the truth of the report that Mr. Smith had lately experienced another narrow escape of another sort. "It is quite true," said Mr. Smith. " Will I tell you about it ? With the greatest pleasure. "I am not one that makes a practice of raising an outcry over my troubles," Mr. Smith continued, " but since you raise the subject, I have no objection to tel you what has happened. There is no fairy tale about it."

"I never expected there was," said the " News ' representative, "and that is the one reason why I desire your unvarnished testimony." Having lit a Pipe. Mr. Smith, a man above the aver- age intelligence of his class, and certainly not given to exaggeration, began his story methodically as follows : —" My home is at 8, Western-buildings,William-street, Hull. For sixteen years 1 have been at sea, and up to the age of twenty-eight enjoyed the best of health. About that time the unusual conditions under which I work began to tell upon my constitution ; and when I say that we are somellmes compell( d to work in a TEMPERAT.,RE AEGISTERING 120 DEGEEES, you may not be surprised that the best of us show signs of premature decay, with an incapacity to per- form our common duties. Apart from this, the noxious fumes caused by burning oil and other substances all , add to the difficulties of our position. Of course I ex- pected to throw ()Ll' the languid fe3ling which I was con- stantly experiencing, but I found I could not do so, and even a turn of fresh air when off duty failed to rid me of the growing indis.„. ositiob. It seemed to get into my very bones, and the na3ty fumes so settled "upon me that I could taste them with my food. I lost my appe- tite,and conseqqently grew rapidly worse. The captain and the rest of my mates could see that there was some- thing radically wrong with me. I sought the advice •f doctors, but not one of them made any difference in my condition. I gradually grew worse. To make life still more miserable I was constintly attacked with neuralgia. The end of it was that I had to remain at home, and quite LOST ALL EXPECTATION OF ESCAPE."

"Had you any pain, Mr. smith ?" "Apart from my neuralgia I cannot say I had. My condition was one of continued weariness. I was lan- guid and lost all interest in everything. Life was in- deed a burden."

" How came you to escape from this new danger? You must Iwo . As MANY LIVES AS A CAT."

"It was through my wife's suggestion. She read of the many cures effected, when ordinary medicine failed, by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pala People. I had been at home a fortnight when I bought the first box." "I suppose you would take a good many boxes before you found any beneficial results." " I thought I should have to, but to my surprise I had scarcely finished the firs,: box when I found life was still worth living, and I returned to my ship. A RAMBLE AFAR.

Br R. E. K.

On a solitary island off the west of Scotland there is a lone little village, were quietly and neacefnlly the inhabitants pass their simple lives free from the harassing cares and worries of competition, free from the grimy smoke and miserable poverty atten- dant on civilisation, out of the struggle for exist- ence. Here in the evening, while walking along the shore after the last rays of a golden sunset had faded away in the darkness of night, one could see —intermittently, in the South, the revolving lights of the Lighthouse on the Island of Rathlin off the North of Ireland ; on the west the lighthouse which served to direct vessels to the village Quay ; and on the east, the lights of the Mull of Cantire could be seen glimmering out their guiding light, while, as the moon rose and silently climbed the vault of heaven, her calm and beautiful light danced on the sparkling ripples of the silvered sea pr, ever and anon, hid herself behind streaks of evening cloud which for a moment veiled the moonbeans from the sight. A walk of five miles from the village, over heather clad hills and bogland, odorous of peat, brought the traveller to a scene which to those who live amidst the great centres of civilisation may well appear very primitive. One cottage stands on the open moorland, and around, here and there, lie ruins of former cottages in which the crofters once had slept ere they were turned out of their quiet home and despatched by the owner of the land to seek their fortunes in a foreign coun- try. The one cottage that remained was small con- sisting of two rooms on the ground floor and was built of rough stone hewn from the rocks which nigh at hand frowned o'er the sea. The roof con- sisted of wooden rafters sparsely thatched over. On entering the cottage one's first thought was that the chimney wanted a cleaning or that the hut was on fire, for the reom was full of the smoke arising from the pro Weeks which burnt in the centre of the ffloor. Tie o;.ly ev'ts for the smoke were a small hole in the roof. an,l the opened door. From the roof hung a chain over the Are, to which was attached a small iron cauldron containing potatoes. Beside the fire, watching the potatoss baking, sat an old man and an old woman. This old couple were a type of a class of poor Highlanders which is fast becoming extinct. They were thoreugly Gaelic in speech and manner. The old woman left the old man to watch the potatoes while she set about pre- paring some large round oatmeal cakes, in a manner that reminded one of the herdsman's wife who was baking cakes when King Alfred sought shelter under her roof.

The cottage standing on en open extent of moor- land, was within two hundred yards of the rugged coast, which was exposed to the full fury of the Atlantic breakers, and about which there were many stories of lives lost, by reason of its treacher- ous nature. The furniture of the cottage consisted of two or three wooden stools or roughly made chairs and the table which was made of plain wood, while the floor was neither paved nor boarded, save that around the fireplace, there were a few rough stones. There was one other room, used as a bedroom, into which a door opened from the room just described. The bedroom was occupied by tlse brother of the old woman who was very feeble and slowly dying. But little attention was apparently paid to him and he was left to pass away the weary and lonely hours as best he cou:d, perhaps with reflections on the monotony and pain- fulness of life, perhaps longing for that change which,ell must hope will bring to the wretched of earth,a happy relief and the blessing of joy eternal. This man has since passed from the toil of this world,to that mystic shore whither all must follow; to that bourne whence none return. The old man and old woman still linger on and continue their simple life, making their own clothes,spinning into a rough cloth the fibres obtained by themselves from certain plants and grasses which grow there- abouts; dyeing their clothes with a juice extracted from a species of sea-weed found on the shore and even moulding some of their dishes. of which they have only a very small stock. Knives and forks and such things are a luxury not used by them. Thefipinning- wheel is evidently one that has been handed down through many generations and, though old, still does its work. The only books the old couple pos- sessed were a Gaelic bible and a Gaelic hymn book and these were the only books ever read by them. They could neither speak nor understand a word of English, but my companion being able to under- stand and speak Gaelic fluently, engaged in an ani- mated conversation with the old woman, which I could not understand. Gaelic, however, sounds very tine when spoken by a native and the feelings seem very beautifully expressed by the lan- guage. The old woman was interrupted in her conversa- tion by the entry of a flock of geese and hens, quacking and cackling in great glee while parading round the room. Thereupon the old woman sent them out and fed them. She makes it a custom to visit the nearest village about once a fortnight to buy in any necessaries which neither she nor her husband can do without. I have it recorded as a noteworthy incident that Estrophael, the old woman, bought a kettle on one of these occasions. It is only on very rare occasions that she parchases anything. The distance she has to walk to the vilhl'e is ove,r.five miles of very bad ground for walking, and one cannot help wondering how these people can possibly exist during the winter when the snows sometimes drift to a depth of six feet and more. The old couple have a certain touch of pride, and are possessed of quite enough means to keep themselves much more comfortably, but grudge spending it. They perhaps enjoy the life they have always been accustomed to, and not having experienced the luxuries of civilisation do not feel the need of them. They have some advant- ages not possossed by millions of men living in our busy centres of industry. They have fresh air; they are natural and surrounded by all that is natural They have enough wholesome food to keep them alive without any of the adulterated pernicious eatables now generally consumed. They are not slaves to drink, they are not like hot-house flowers but rather like wild plants which thrive best when left free and unconfined. They are healthy ani though the old man and old woman and formerly tthe woman's brother all slept in the same room, they are not so shut up from the light of the sun and the sight of nature as are the sons of toil existing in our great manufacturing towns. They know nothing of the world outside their little hut, save the very little news the old women gets when she makes a trip to the village—which is itself but little affected—and knows but little of the great developments of civilisation. They have no idea of the many varieties and the many vicissi- tudes of the outer world. They know not or doubts arising from modern enlightenment and live in con- tentment with a simple faith in their only books, the bible and the hymn book. The many struggles going on in the world with its failures and disasters, with its successes and its victories, are hot for them while they are quite oblivious of the enlightening influence of education.

It is not unpleasent to find that in Great Britain there still remain a few unfrequented and isolated spots,around which still hangs the odour of anti- quity, and where are people who yet ietnain un- touched by the great waves of advancement. Jr is interesting to look at these relics of the past which the huge wave has left high and dry. as yet, but which,by the rapidly rising tide of progress, must, ere long.be washed away and engulfed in the ever active strife of.competition. I have given one true example of' these out-of-the-world scenes still to be found, which lingeringly await their development by the prowess of the manufacturer and the compe- tition of commerce.

SOME important and interesting evidence was given. on Wednes- day by Mr. W. CROSFIRLD before the Royal Commission on the working of. the licensing laws. Mr. CROSFIELD'S evidence, of course, related more particularly to Liverpool, but it has a direct application to Warrington and other similiar towns. On the subject, for instance,. of magistrates " bartering" licenses with brewers,• what Mr. CROSFIRLD had to say applied with fully as much force, and perhaps more, to Warrington as to Liverpool. In Liverpool the attempts to' exchange several unprofitable, or at least less profitable, old licenses for one very profitable nevi one have, it appeared, been failures; in Warring- ton one such transaction at least has been taken. part in by the local bench. Mr. CROSFIELDOWRS' asked if he saw any objection in principle "to the bargaining for the suppression of five or sixhouses for the erection of one ?" His reply was prompt and emphatic. Such a principle. he said, was 0- " most pernicious" one, and in this opin- ion he will be supported by most people' who have given the subject serious and unbiassed consideration. Magistrates run s' serious risk of degrading the responsible office which they hold when they come down to the. bar- gaining or bartering business With anybody who. appears before them. And, besides, as Mr. CROS' YIELD said, the point which magistrates should consider when a new license is applied for is the' need of the district in which it is proposed to locate the license. If the people of, that district. do not want the licensed house in their vicinity it is neither more or less than odious tyranny W' force it upon them. Another objection to the bartering of-licenses is the fact that it is, always' the brewer and not the public who stands to gain. by the arrangement. Mr. CROSFIELD put this side' of the question very appositely and concisely. 10 was asked if the proposal to surrender ha-wdozen licenses was not a tempting offer., andi replied, "Undoubtedly, but L think it would be reasonable to suppose that the licenses that were being surrendered were licens3s which have lost or are losing their value, and that in the cedheari course of business the individual who makes the offer will before very long find it to his interest" to close the house." The accuracy of the state' mint is, broadly spoaking„ self-evident. When firm.of brewers offers these exchanges of licensee, it does not do so with the object of losing by the exchange. The owning of publis-houses I's a buff hese, the end,and abject of which is moiler making. Some people may. and possr.ibly with some show of reason, argue that their business° of_ a philanthropic or quasi-philanthrivic chars° ter, but brewers do not and. can notargue in th° way 1..;,1: nil.; should, therefore., per-emptora decline to allow themselves to be made tll9 medium of a profitable "deal," no matter unclef what plausible guise it nsay he. presented to thenii; Mr. W. CROSFIELD also dealt in his evidence Wlt the" restricted "districts in Liverpool, that ie say the districts in which there are no pubh(re 'houses awing to the fact that the owners of 0., land will not allow public-houses to be opened (76 their property. to a restricted area is to be seen in the improved PilYsical and moral condition of the people. "The change in their surroundings has," says Mr. CROSFIELD, "brought about an improvement in their condition, so that they can hardly be recog- rased as the same people." The magistrates of Liverpool have—let it be said to their credit— Protected the inhabitants of the restricted dis- tricts. There is a great desire on the part of brewers to establish public-houses on the horde s of these districts, but in every instance the magis trates have refused the applications for licenses, although offers have been made to give up five or six old licenses a the new one. One Other subject was touched upon by Mr. CROS- FIELD which will awaken a sympathetic Iceling in the breasts of all temperance reformers—that was the power of appeal in licen- sing cases from local magistrates to county magis- trates at Quarter Sessions. Mr. CROSFIELD con- demns this arrangement as most unsatisfactory in regard to the cities, and,he might have added,the towns. This is really a system of appeal from those who know to those who do not know. The local magistrates have a full knowledge of the character and requirements of the localities for which they act, and in addition to that, they are in a sense responsible to the people amongst whom they live ; yet under the present system of appeal their decisions may be upset by a number of county gentlemen, who are not nearly as well qualified in point of knowledge to pass judgment. It is to be hoped that if the R3yal Commission !eads to anything in the way of licensing reform one of its accomplishments will be the abolition of appeals to Quarter Sessions.

It is to be feared that even the Mr. Balfour at Rochdale most uncritical Tory will find that the speech which Mr. BA.LFOUB, de- livered at Rochdale on Tuesday is of an extremely unsatisfying nature. Mr. BALFOUR is popularly supposed to be the most important man in the present Government aaer Lord SALISBURY; he is the leader of the House of Commons, and he is one of the "inner ring" of the Cabinet, yet there is nothing in his speech that might not have been said by the most inGdest of under s3cretaries, or even by a Conservative who holds no official posi- tion whatever. We do not know whetly3r the Government has any definite policy or not, but if it has Mr. BALFOITR does not reveal it. It is true that, on domestic subjects something was said about the education question. Mr. BALFOUR, did not say what the G ivernment, intended to do; he did say, however, what they intended not to do. They are not going to attempt to pass any big Education Bill next year. In that direc- tion they evidently intend to confine their atten- tion chielly,if not entirely, to the giving of further assistance out of public fund to the voluntary schools. But whatever they do they will do piec3meal and in separate Bills. The last Education Bill was too large and complex for Mr. BALFona,and in the debates upon it he cut only a poor figure in com- parison with Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT and the other critics of the Bill on the Opposition side of the House. When speaking on foreign topics, Mr. BALFOUR was equally vague and indefinite. His references to Armenia were in the usual Tory vein —that is to say, he depreciated the power of this country, and denied that the Arntenians had any special claim upon us. It is an ignoble positio nto take up, considering that one of the conditions of the Cyprus Convention was a special undertaking to look after the welfare of the Christians of Turkey. A leading Russian newspaper, in refer- ring to Lord SALISBURY'S Guildhall speech says " there stands at the head of the British Govern- ment a man not possessing sufficient strength of spirit to inspire new faith and life into the morally- dejected people of England." There was no in spiration in Lord SALISBURY'S speech, and there is none in Mr.BALFOUR'S.
