ODDFELLOWSHIP AT WARRINGTON

JUVENILE BURIAL —SOCIETY.—PRESENTATION TO THE LATE SECRETARY. The Board of Directors of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows Juvenile Burial Society(Warrington District) met at the White Bull, Hersemarket street, on Monday evening, for the purpose ef making a presentation of a rumble timepiece to Bro. Pownall, late secretary, who severed his connection with. the Board a short time ago owing to his leaving the town. Before the presentation the members sat down to a rabbit pie supper. Bro. W. ch irman of the Board, presided over the proceed- ings, and in calling on Bro. Cross, one of the trustees,to make the presentation, said they all knew for what pur- pose they met that night,which was to make a presenta- tion of a marble timepiece to their respected late secre- tary, Bro. J. Pownall. He hoped the present would be handed down as an heirloom in Bro. Pownall's family. Bro. CROSS, in making the presentation which it may be stated was supplied by Messrs. E. and A. Eustance, Sankey-street, said he could assure them that he consi- dered it an honour to have been aelted to make the pre- sentation to Bro. Pownall. They owed much to the efforts of their brother,who had always striven to make the society what it was at the present time. When he (tho speaker) first came on the Board Bro. Powitall oc- cupied the position of vice-chairman, a positinn he ful- filled with honour to himself and credit to the Board.and the Board recognised his services when they appointed him to the honourable position of chairman. Afterwards he was appointed in turn to the position of treasurer and secretary, which he also occupied with every credit to himself and the society. Be (Bro. Cross) hoped the timepiece would be received by the members of Bro. Pownall's family as a memento of the esteem in which he was held by the members of the society. In conclu- sion he hoped Bro. Pownall would live to see the clock record many happy years. Bre. JOHN APPLETON spoke in high terms of praise of the services Bro. Pownall had rendered to the society. He hoped their brother would value the gift not for its intrinsic value, but as a mark of the great esteem in which he was held by the Board. The VICE-CHAIRMAN (Bro. Savory) said during the time he had sat on the Board,Bro. Pownall had been an officer worthy of esteem. He regretted the circumstances which called forth the presentation. He was glad to see Bro. Pownall again with them, and hoped he would speedily be restored to geod health. Bro. JOYNSON appropriately referred to the services Bro. Pownall had rendered the society, and gave a brief resume of the growth of the society sines its inauguration in 1868. The first year's working of the society resulted in their placing £27 15s. 3.1 d. to their credit, and now. after 28 years had gone by, they were worth £1,552 Ss. 81,which was invested as follows :—ln the Corpora- tion, £l,OOO ; Warrington Savings Bank, £240; Medical Association,£lso ; Warrington District of the G.U.0.0., £lOO ; lodge Inan,,£so ; in treasurer's hands. £l2 belt In all they pad 482 death claims, and they had now a membership of 941. Much of this pcpularity had been brought about by the efforts of Bro. Pownall and his brother officers. Bro. POWNALL, who was deeply moved,in responding, said the present bed taken him by surprise. He had no idea that in doing what was only his duty he had earned the approval of the Board more than any of the other officers. S';11 he wos proud to think the members appre- ciated his .1 ,rte on behalf of the society. Ele well remembered 11-4 commencement, and he might claim to have been coenected with it since then, as in 1169 he entered one of hi- children as a juvenile. In 1874 he was elected a delegate, --, as it was now called, a director, and ever since it ha.: been his ambition to see the ex- penses of their collectors covered by the interest from their securities. He was glad to notice in the last balance sheet this had been accomplished, as their in- terest amounted to £4O, while the collectors' expense 4 were only £3B odd. He again thanked them ane and all for their valuable present, which he could assure them would be highly treasured by him and the members of his family. The rest of the evening was spent in a convivial man- ner, and votes of thanks brought the proceedings to a close.

A young man named John Rowell, a leadminer, of Rock-hope, in Weardale, in company with four others, Walked about eight miles over the fells en Saturday to 'witness the Braes of Derent hounds. When returning they were overtaken by darkness and fog, and were lost to each other. Only four grrivecl their destination. On Sunday unavailing search of the Fells was made, but on Monday a search party of over 100 men was organised, and the dead body of the missing man was found on tile Fells.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, before Mr. J. Brundret and Mr. T. Rigby, the following persons were each fined 53. for neglecting to send their children regularly to school :—Thomas James, Surrey-street ; Edward Wainwright,Robert-Areet ; Job Nield, Handley- street.

MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. The usual weekly meeting of the Brunswick Mutual Improvement Siety was held on Wednesday evening in the Brunswick schoolroom. Mr. E. Simister, presided, and there was a large attendance of members. Mr. Bighnell read a paper on gambling. During the course of his paper, he condemn I the practice most atrongly. A discussion followed.

DRUNKENNESS.

Two Norwegian sailors, named Eunda Abrahsen, and Inoras Gustavisen,belouging to the Ship Petropolis,were charged before Mr. F. J. Norman and Mr. Taylor, at the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesd4y morning, with be- ing drunk and disorderly in Percival-lane the previous night. P.C. Lakin proved the case, and they were each fined 51. and 53. 4d. costs.—Before Mr. J. W. Lightburn on Thursday morning, Herbert Barlow was fined 53. and coats for being drunk in Cooper-street on Wednesday night.

ACCIDENT TO A RUNCORN FOOTBLLLER.

On Saturday afternoon, during the match between Runcorn Recreatian and Broughton Recreation, on tin ground of the former, an accident happened to T. Sum- merfield,of Weston. He was charging the centre three- quarter of the opposing team when they both fall to the ground. Qn rising it was found that the local player had sustained serious injuries. He was as quickly as possible attended to by Dr. MacLennan,who on examin- tion pronounced it a serious case of fractured collar bone.

COLLISION AT RUNCORN Dock

On Thursday morning the steamship Valde Travers, 500 tons burden, belonging to J. Harris, proceeding to Manchester from St. Valeri with flints, collided with the pierhead at Runcorn docks when enterieg the tidal basin. The force of the impact was very great aa the ship was coming up the Ship Canal on a high fl )od tide, but mar- vellously escaped serious damage. After discharging,the ship is proceeding to Manchester to be overhauled. CONCERT TOR THE WORKHOUSE INMATES.

Miss Rigby, of the Cedars, Sutton, along with a num- ber of ladies and gentlemen gave a concert at Dutton Workhouse,on Saturday afternoon. Mr. J. M.Willianas, one of the Guardians for Frodsham, presided, and the Programme consisted of songs, recitations, &c. The con- cert was much appreciated by the inmates. Hearty votes of thanks were given to the fellowing : Mrs. Rodger,Miss Rodger,Misss Alice Rigby,Miss Mr. Lewis, and Mr. Williams, for having provided such a pleasant entertainment. A vote o thanks was also passed to Mr. and Mrs. Cunnah, (master and matron)for their trouble.

DROWNING MYSTERY AT ASTON. An inquest was held at A.ston-by.:Sutton by Mr. J. E. Worslev, coroner, on Tuesday, on the body of William Henry Hart, gardener, Parkside Farm. Deceased, whose much-decomposed body was recovered from the river Weaver, had been missing since October 19, when he left The Boat public-house, Crowton, at tan o'clock at night, quite fresh. On his way home he had to walk a short distance along the riverside. Verdict, "Found drowned."

INTERESTING PRES/INTATION AT RUNCORN.

On Tuesday evening Mr. R. Foulkes,of Frodshamoras the recipient of a massive gold albert guard, with pen- dant,on which is engraved : "Presented by a few well wishers connected with Camden Works,Runcorn, to Mr. Robert Foulkes, September, 1896 ;" on the reverse is en- graved "A square and compass, crown, Rev. ii. 10." He was also presented with four valuable books, viz., Bishop J. B. Lightfoot's Commentary on Philippians, Lane on Modern Egypt, Rev. Adolph Saphir's lectures, Latimer's •Lifo by Rev. R. Demans, M.A. The above were given along with a cheque. Mr. Foulkes returned his very greatful thanks to the subscribers,some of whom have known him during the vrJole period of thirty years in which he has beea engaged at the above soap works as yard manager,shipping and confidential clerk, and traveller.

FUNERAL OF AN OLD RUNCORNIAN. On Saturday afternoon, the remains of William Hitch- mough, aged 81 years, were interred in the burial ground connected with the Runcorn Parish Church. The de- ceased, who died at the residence of his nephew, Mr. George Howard, South Bank-terrace, on Wednesday night, was one of the oldest Oddfelfows in the district. He assisted in the establishment of the first lodge in Runcorn of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows,Man- ch -ster Unity. and has occupied several offices. For a great many years, prior to the construction of the L. and N.W. Railway bridge across the Mersey, he was the manager of the Ferry,the only means of communication between Widnes and Runcarn. After the erection ot the bridge he returned to his former employment, namely, sail making, which he relinquished only some five years ago. Deceased was a staunch conservative and a Churchman. He was followed nc grave by Messrs. Robert Foulkes, John Foulkes (Liverpool), John Davies, Cringhall, George Howard and William Howard. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. W. H. Stables.

RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANOR.

At the fortnightly meeting of this association, on Sat- urday night, in the Greenway-road School, under the presidency of Mr. George Holt, an interesting paper was read by Mr. Edward Key, on "Temperance efforts prior to the dawn of the modern temperance reform." After dwelling on the evils of intemperance he referred to the various efforts made before the present reform, com- mencing in 1828, to stem the tide of intemperance. He read a copy of a pledge taken by several Egyptian priests 4,000 years ago, and also referred to the history of the Nazarites. Mr. Key mentioned the names of the Bible worthies who were abstainers from drink. In closing his paper he alluded to the noble stand made by the Preston pioneers, Joseph Livesley, Thomas Swindle- burst, and ethers, all working men. After the reading of the paper there was a discussion, in which the follow- ing took part :—Messrs. George Holt, Daniel Bisbruwn, James Johnson, Thomas Clarke, and Thomas J. Holt. The speakers referred to the noble stand taken by veterans in the cause in their; own town, special refer- ence being made to the work accomplished by the late Mr. William Holt, a framed portrait of whom was on view, which had been presented to him (Mr. Holt) at a public meeting in the year 1842 by his co-workers in the cause, to slaow their appreciation of his services. An interesting meeting was then brought to a close, after a a vote of thanks had been accorded to Mr. Key f)r his paper.

RUNCORN TOLUNTEERS.

PRIZE DISTRIBUTION BY LADY BROOKE.

The annual prize distribution in connection with th Runcorn Volunteers, took place on Monday night in the Public Hall.Runcorn. The evening's entertainment con- sisted cf a grind concert and dance. The proceedings were openeu by the band of the local corps playing a selection frem " Lucrezia Borgia!' Mr. Kennerley Rumford, baritone, of the Crystal Palace, and St. James' Nall concerts,who is so well known as a baritone singer, excellently rendered" Fair is my love," which was well received by the audience. " Asthore" (Darling) was rendered excellently, by Miss Gertrude Izard,soprano,of the principal London concerts a d Norwich festival. The well-known numorist,Mr. Leslie Ilarris,was greeted with a loud outburst of applause, when he made his ap- pearance on the stage. He rendered a humoreus sketch entitled" The gentle art of courting." He was loudly recalled. The distribution of prizes then took place. On the stage, Lady and Sir Richard Brooke, Colonel Ashton, Colonel Rich, (South Lancashire Regiment), Captain Burton, adjutant, Majors Smith, Harrison, and Dutton, Captain Evans, Lieutenants, Bromley, Burton,Brundritt, and Ashton and the Rev. Dr. Preston' (chaplain). Captain G. F. Ashton, commanding the Runcorn de- tachment, said that before the prizes were distributed, he on behalf of the Runcorn Volunteers thanked the donors once more for assisting them. The report of the ' Runcorn detachment for the past season was a very good one. They numbered 150. as compared with 148 the previous year. That was certainly not quite as good as they anticipated. He expressed a hope that the in- crease next year would be far greater. As regards effi- ciency, he could say that the detachment had held its own in every particular. There was a marked improve- ment in the class firing. Last year the figure of merits was 70; this year it averaged 94 per man, which showed an increase of ever 30 per cent. The attendance at drill had also iscreased, and their camping engage- ments this year had been very satisfactory indeed. Out ot the 150 members they took to camp 148 ; only four men out of both companies being absent from the encampment. They considered that good and wished for nothing better. The men whilst at camp behaved splendidly. Altogether there were nine companies at camp, Runcorn being composed of two. Prizes were given for smartnese, and out of the nine prizes given the Runcorn men were able to secure five. (Applause.) Their one drawback this year was the less et their rile range. But they were now expecting to get one in the neighbourhood.

Ashton commanding, 2nd Earl of Chester Battalion,said he was sure that they would agree with him in giving their best thanks to Sir Richard and Lady Brooke,for their kindness in coming that night to dis- tribute the prizes. Lady Brooke took a great interest in volunteers.and the late Sir Richard Brooke, Bart., had command of a volunteer regiment. He could end else every word Captain Ashton had said, and not a finer company there need be. They were a sample of the rest of the regiment. Before proposing a vote of thanks, he would ask Lady Brooke to present a certificate to Lance- Corporal Houghton, granted to him by the Royal Humane Society, for having saved the life of one of the members of a Welsh regiment whilst in camp at To wyn. A. number of the men were bathing in the sea after drill, and whilst Lance-Corporal Houghton was walking along the beach, he noticed a man in difficulty. Without a moment's hesitation he threw off his tunic, and went to assist the drowning comrade. Great credit was due to their member who at the risk of his own life-as he could not swim-rescued the Welsh member. The affair was reported to the Royal Humone Society, and they in return for his timely act, rewarded him with a certifi- cate. Lady Brooke then delivered the certificate to Corporal Houghton amid loud applause. The Rev. Dr. Preston (chaplain) ,seconded the proposi- tioe which was agreed to. Sir Richard Brooke, replying on behalf of himself and Lady Brooke, said he thanked them very muck for the reception given to him and his wife that night. They had always taken a great interest in the volunteer forces. He expected if the 2nd Cheshire Battalion were called upon, they would render a good account of themselves. In conclusion, Sir Richard promised to give a cup, to be competed for by the local corps. The musizal part of the programme was then con- tinued. A dance programme consisting of 14 dances,was afterwards submitted to the company present, the regimental band supplyirg the music.

RUNCORN FREE fUBLIC LIBRARY.- PROPOSED BOOK CLUB.

The monthly meeting of the committee of the Runcorn Free Public Library was held on Tuesday evening in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall. Mr. S. Roper presided.—lne Chairman presented a report respecting the proposed Book Club. The sub-committee, he said, are of opinion that if a fair number of subscribers—say not less than fifty—could be obtained, that it would result in the purchase of many new books, as they issue from the Press, and which would automatically and regularly add to and enrich the library. The advantage to the library and the general public would be very material. The sub- committee thought that as one means of ascertaining if there is a desire for the establishment of a book club,the further consideration could with advantage be postpobed for a few weeks, so as to afford opportunity for any who would possibly become members to communicate with the secretary and librarian."—The report was adc pted.

The autumnal Convention in connection with the Warrington and District Association of Young People's Societies of Christian Endeavour was held in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Earlestown, on Thursday. WARRINGTON TECHNICAL INSTITUTE. The following report by H. M. Inspector to the Education Department on the work of the Con- tinuation School for 1895-6 has recently been received :—" The School has been taught with gratifying success. With well directed efforts the instruction has been made practical and thorough, and secured the interest of a largemumber of pupils, who, by their quiet demeanour and evident earnest- ness, have contributed in great measure to the very satisfactory standard of proficiency attained."

LANTERN SERVICES AT WARRINGTON. The Rev. W. H. Prummond, 8.A., resumed his lantern services in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick- st., on Sunday evening. There was a large audience, , and the rev, gentleman read the interesting narra- tive of the "Prodigal Son." The narrative was profusely illustrated by lantern slides, Mr. Drum- mond explaining each picture as it appeared on the screen. A hymn was sung, after which the solo, "The Lord is mindful of His own," was rendered. Then followed the story of the life of Jesus Christ, which was graphically told, and the interest of the narrative enhanced by the pictures. The audience were taken from the birth of our Lord to His Cruci- fixion. The well-known solo "Crossing the Bar," from the lines of Lord Tennyson, was sung. The lantern service was much enjoyed, and they will be continued during the winter.

SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN.

GRAND NA.O FICAL BAZAAR. A grant nautical bazaar was onened on Wednesday by Lady Brooke, in the Public He e Zuncorn, for the pur- pose at raising funds to clee I the debt upon the Seaman's Institute, Runcorn. free cost of the building itself, was £l,2o(lfkbut this sum had been defrayed by the generous co-operation of many friends, but the cost of the site, £4OO, still remained a debt, and with the object of wiping out that sum, the committee of the Runcorn Seamen's Institute decided to hold a three days bazaar. The following particulars illustrate the long felt need of a Seamen's Mission Church and Institute in Runcorn. Previous to the opening of the Mission Room at Bottom Locks In the year 1875, there was a small Mis- sion Church called St. Peter's, supported by the late Countess of Ellesmere, served by a curate with surpliced choir, etc., under the charge of the late Vicar, Canon Barclay. When the Bridgewater trustees transferred the Canal property to the B. N. Co.,' Ltd., St. Peter's was closed for a considerable time, and remained so until 1875, when the late Mr. Thomas Beddows, the resident agent of the N. B. Co. ,together with Rev. Canon Barclay aud Dr. Preston, thought that it was necessary that a Mission Room should be opened for tee use of the float- ing population, which was incree leg rapidly. Accord- ingly communications were eoened with the Mersey Mission to Seamen at Liverpeol,and an application made to the committee of that society to send a missionare to Runcorn. In the event of this baing done the *cove- named three gentlemen promised to place e 2: feter's Church at their disposal for the use of a mission. Tha Mersey Mission Committee acceded to this request and appointed Mr. W. Shaw as their missionary, and in 1875 Mr. Shaw commenced his labours at the port. In course or time the trade of the port increased so much, and the work of the Mission progressed so rapidly that the need of larger premises became aoparent. The old room was incapable of enlargement and the extension of the docks rendered it unsuitable. Fortunately a very suitable free- hold site in Station-road, Runcorn, near the locks was acquired and the present handsome building was erected on it. The ceremony of laying the foundation stone of Hie new Institute was performed by the Right Hon. Lord Egerton of Tatton on April Mt, 1891, and was opened on the 10th December in the same year, by the Right Rev, the Lord Bishop of Chester.—The oeening ceremony was performed on Wednesday by Lady Brooke, before a large company. The Rev. Edgar Lambert, M.A.. chaplain to the Insti- tute,said he had been aeked ta perform the needless task ef introducing to them One who was very much known, and to many of them better known than himself. Lady Brooke had made herself popular by her many acts of kindness to the Seamen's Mission, and he was sure when she opened the proceedings that afternoon, she wished them success. Lady Brooke said she was pleased to be present that afternoon, and was very glad to help such a good move- ment. The Runcorn Seamen's Mission sp eke for itself, and thereforo it needed no recommendation. Twelve hundred pounds had already been raiseA by the friends of sailors in Runcorn and elsewhere. This sum had de- frayed the actual cost of the Institute,and now only the site remained to be paid for. It needed one final effort to free the mission, which had done some excellent work in the past, and which had proved a boon to so many seamen. She felt sure that the appeal which the com- mittee were making on behalf of the Institute would be responded to in a joyous and hearty manner, so that the committee could announce to them that the required sum had been raised. She had now great pleasure in declar- ing the bazaar open, and she wished it success. Captain Heasley, superintendent of the Manchester Ship Canal (Runcorn section), proposed a vote of th inks to Lady Brooke for her kindness in coming to open the bazaar. 13e was confident that it would be a great success Mr. J. Digges, hon. secretary, seconded the pro- position and said that Lady Brooke had always taken a deep interest in the Seamen's Mission. She had at ways helped it forward, even when she was not asked to do so. (Applause.) Mr. W. Shaw (missionary) supported the proposition, and said that during the 20 years which he had been in Runcorn he never remembered seeing the Public Hall looking so smart as it did that day. (Laughter.) After the bazaar,he hoped they would be able to wipe out the debt and start afresh with a free hand. The Rev. E. Lambert said a deep sense of gratitude was due to those who so splendidly and assiduously worked together, in order to produce such a beautiful and most useful result, whereby £4OO was expected to be realised. They were extremely grateful to Lady Brooke for the very kind, and thoughtful words she had uttered that afternoon. Their heartiest thanks were due to her, and when this debt had been paid off, and she saw the mission going ahead prospering it would con- tinue to give her joy, that she had helped it forward en that occasion, and many previous ones. Three hearty cheers were given for Lady Brooke. Master Richard Christopher Brooke replied. The stalls were beautifully decorated, and represented the different steamers, and steamship companies. THE ALLE4ED MURDER AT WARRINGTON. HEARING AT LIVERPOOL ASSIZES.

On Thursday in the Crown Court at the Liverpool Assizes, before Mr. Justice Bruce, the tragedy which startled Warrington in the early days of September formed the first subject of inquiry. The aceused, James Bate a labourer, thirty-two years of age, was indicted for having at Warrington, between the 7th and Bth Sep- tember, feloniously, willfully, and of malice afore- thought killed and murdered Catherine, his wife. Mr. Coßingwood Hope and Mr. Lancelot Saunderson (in- structed by Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk of War- rineton)conducted the proseeution,the priscner being de- fended by Mr. M'Keand and Mr. M'Keever (instructed by Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins). THE SPEECH FOR THE PROSECU HON. Mr. Collingwood Hope, opening the ease for the pro- secution, said that the prisoner was charged with the wilful murder of Catherine Bate, who was his wife, and with whom he had lived for some fourteen years at Warrington. The charge was of wilful murder on the 7th or in the early morning of the Bth of September of this year. Prisoner was a chemical worker, and both he and his wife were both persons addicted to drink. He would only give the jury shortly the various events which took place on the two dates named. The prisoner in the ordinary course, went to work on the morning of the 7th,leaving his wife and the little girl Maggie in the house. He returned in the evening at six o'clock from his work. Earlier in the day the deceased woman had gone to the louse of a neighbour named Connor. She, with other women, as he believed was their practice, was drinking beer more or less throughout the day. The picture was a sad one for squalor and drunkenness. The little girl Maggie came into the house at four o'clock, and, finding her mother out, went to Mrs. Connor's house, where she found her drinking with other women. Maggie went home, and afterwards left the ho-se again. The prisoner returned at six o'clock, and found that there was no food prepared for him, and no preparation whatever made for his return. The house was untidy and just as he left it in the morning. He asked his daughter Maggie where her mother was, and she told him she was at Mrs. Connor's. He went to Pinner's-brow where the house was, and alt lough the little girl did not know as a fact that he went to Mrs. r3onnor's, they knew from his own statement that he did go there, and saw his wife drinking with the other women. He thereupon seemed to have taken drink himself, and to have become drunk during the course of the evening. At 8.30 ceceased was seen under the influence of drink and to fall forward and roll on her back. At nine o'clock deceased was quarell- ing with a woman,but at 10.30 p.m. she went home,and with the little girl Maggie went to bed, lying on a pallet bed in a back bedroom. That house, and in fact all the houses of which that formed one,were of a most miser- able description. They were badly built, and not only could talking be heard from one house to another' but there were holes in the wall through which the people could see from one house to another... On the pallet .bed deceased and

her daughter lay. About eleven o'clock prisoner came home drunk. He was seen leaning against the wall of the next house by Mrs. Fearnley, who assisted him into his own house. He called Mrs. Fearnlev's attention to the disorderly condition of his house, and then made use of an expression towards his wife that made Mrs. Fearn- ley run out. He then lit a candle and went up into the bedroom where his wife and his daughter lay. Be asked his wife if she was coming into the next room. She said she was not. He then knelt down and thumped her on the face many times. He also kicked her in the mouth. He was wearing boots tipped with iron. Then he kicked her in the stomach. She turned over apparently to pro- tect herself from his violence,and he stamped on her back. At this time her head appeared to have been hanging over the pallet bed,and taking her by the back hair he thumped her head against the door many times with the result that her head was bruised and bent in and her forehead pulpified. Blood flowed freely,and next morning a pool, a toot square, was found on the floor. There were also found seventeen splashes of blood. While prisoner was thumping her head against the floor she screamed, the little girl and the little boy, who was also in the room, also screamed,and entreated their father not to "la.y"on their mother. The mother also entreated him to desist. All these sounds were heard by neighbours, who heard the ejaculation "Oh "some four times, after- wards a gurgling noise, and then, as far as the woman was concerned,all sounds died away. Prisoner took his little girl into the front bedroom, and on the way said, " Mageie, I have always been a good daddy to you, haven't I?" The little girl replied." Yes daddy." The little girl slept with him all night until about nine o'clock next morning, when she got up and went into the back bedroom for a skirt. Upon her return she told her father that her mother was " lying white" in the other room. Prisoner went into the other room, and sending his daughter for a cloth and some water,wiped the blood off his dead wife's face, and partly from the floor. After doing this he threw the cloth away. He said to Maggie " You must not tell anybody that I "laid" on your mother.lf you do I shall get locked up. Tell them that she fell down- stairs, or that she was in the yard and fell down." The little girl did make these statements to several. Prisoner was arrested in Buckley-street that night. He informed the officer that he had not seen his wife from the time he saw her at Connor's until his daughter awoke him at eight o'clock that morning, when his daughter said she was looking very white. The result of the post- mortem examination showed that death was due to effu- sion of blood on the brain, caused by direct violence attributable to the violence of the bumping of the fore- head. If the case against the prisoner was proved to the satisfaction of the jury they must return a verdict of wilful murder, however rainful it might be to them to do so, but if, on the other hand, they thought it had not , been proved, then it was their duty, and it would be a pleasure to them, to acquit him. John Sutcliffe, assistant borough surveyor, stated that he made an examination of the house, 11. Oliver-street, Warrington, on the 10th Septeriaber, and on other dates. The plans produced were prepared by him. The house consisted of four rooms, two below and two above. It was an old and dilapidated house. The outside front and back walls were bulged and settled. That had caused a space at either end of the party wall for a ver- tical length of three feet in the upper rooms. The space in the front room between the party wall *as about one inch. Witness stood in the house, No 11, in company with Inspector Spinks, and Sergeant Cheney in No. 9. He saw an object held hy Set geant Cheney in No, 9 from the back bedroom of No. 11. Witness took a measure- ment of the party wall which was 0 inches thick. The party wall ; did net reach the ceiling by about sixteen inches.—ln answer to a juryman. witness said it was a brick wall. Above the wall was lath and plaster. Portions of this plaster was broken. There was a hole in the ceiling of the front' room of No. 9, about 18 inches from the party wall. They also made experiments with regard to sound in both houses. Standing in Ne. 9he spoke to Sergeant Cheney in No. 11. He could hear the Sergeant's voice and understand what he said. He spoke in an ordinary tone, and received correct answers tolthe questions put. He made the same experiments in No. 11. The voice i was fainter, but distinctly audible. The experiments were made about five o'clock in the afternoon. He found bloodstains in the back bedroom of No. 11. The blood stain was 15 in. by 12 in. There were splashes of blood on the wall between 11 and 13. The positions of the blood stains were marked correctly on the plan produced —Cross-examined by Mr. McKeaud, witness said the bloodstains had been wiped up, but not obliter- ated. Asked whether, when before the magistrates, he mentioned anything about the splashes of blood, he ree plied: Ido not rernehber whether they were mentioned or mot ; they were on the plan.— The Judge: The plans were in evidence ?—Witness: They were my lord.— Mr. McKeand : Although that plan was before the magistrates with red specks on they never asked what those red specks meant P—Witness : They did not ask me what they meant ?—Mr. McKean : Did you attempt to explain what they meant ? Witness: I think they maderstood.—Mr. McKeand : There are red ink marks here (on the plan). Was not the question asked what they meant ?—Witnese : It was probably explained by the prosecuting solicitor.— Mr. McKeand : I contend that it was not explained, and it is the first time you have ever told as about these red specks. Igo a bit further : how far was it from the pallet to the nearest side of I these splashes ? Witness : One foot nine inches. They were immediately above the actual pool of blood. Margaret Bate, daughter of the prisoaer,the principal witness, said she remembered the 11th September. She went home at four o'clock in the afternoon. Her mother was at Connor's, a neighbour. She went to her mother there, and she was drinking beer at the house. Her mother often went there. She said to her mother, "Come home,and get my father's tea ready." She sent her home, and she washed some of the things up. Her father came home at six o'clock. We asked her where her mother was. She replied at "Connor's." Her father then went to her grandmother Bate's. She next saw her mother at l'inner's-brow. She was with her Aunt Mary, Mrs. Buckley. She did not see any blows struck. Her mother came home afterwards. She was drunk. Witness then went out and got some potato chips. Her- self and brother John ate them. Her brother was four years of age. After supper her mother told her that when her father came he would kill her. She went to bed in the back bedroom, and her mother came in the same bed. Her mother slept nearest the fireplace. rler father came into the room some time afterwards with a candle. He asked her mother be go in the other room. She did not do so, and he then knelt down on the bedroom iloor and thumped her mother with his fist. He kicked her on the head and stomach. Her mother said" Maggie" three times. She also called" Kitty," and said to her (witness's) father " don't kill me and I will give you twopence halfpenny for a pint of beer." Her father said that he intended to kill her. Her mother turned over on her face, and her father then stamped with his foot on her mother's back. He then got hold of her hair and banged her head on the floor about four times. Witness was in bed crying because her father said he would kill her mother. She afterwards went with her father to the front bedroom.—By the Judge :

Was your father dressed ?—Yes.—What had he on his feet P—His boots.—What became of your brother and sister; did they remain in bed with your mother or go in the other room with you P—They came along with me. —By Mr. C. Hope : What did your father say when taking you into the other room ?-11e 'Said " Have I not been a good father to you." The next morning she went fer her skirt to the next bedroom,and saw her mother white. elle went back to her father and told him " Daddy my mammy is white." She didn't notice how her mother lay. Her father said "Is she," and he then went jute the other room. He told her to go downstairs and get some water and a cloth. She did so, and her father wiped the blood from her mother's face, and also wiped the blood up from the floor. He told her before he went out " Maggie don't tell anybody I kicked her; because I shall get locked up. Tell them she fell downstairs." Her mother did not get out of bed to go downstairs that night. Her mother was drunk, but she did not notice anything else about her. She did not see any marks about her face at all. She had since been in a home in Liverpool.—Cross- examined by Mr. McKeand, she said her mother was al- ways getting drunk, and when she was drunk she was noisy and given to fighting. Her father had not been good to the children. When prisoner came home de- ceased was drinking at Connor's, and there was no tea for him. He had gone away in the morning without breakfast, because her mother had been drunk the day before. When prisoner thumped deceased the latter ex- claimed" Oh Jim !" Then, when he kicked her she did not scream. Prisoner stamped one of his feet on de- ceased's back. She did not know whether he used both feet. Her father had not been cruel to her. When she went into the bedroom next morning, and saw that her mother was leoking white, she knew that 'something had happened." Her father looked very much put out. and went to see how her mother was. It wee witness who "chucked" the flannel away, and not her father. She was quite sure her father told her to say that the de- ceased had fallen downstairs. She heard her father say that he was in a bit of trouble. He was crying, and appeared to be very ITIRCII "put out." Nothing was said about her mother having been pashed down the stairs. At first witness admitted having said that her father had not beaten deceased that night. That was said to elrs.Wheelan. She told her grandmother that her mother had fallen downstairs. She remembered seeing Sergeant Cheney, who asked her several questions about the matter. He had spoken to her many times about it. Other persons had spoken to her also. While at the Liverpool home she had been spoken to by a policeman, who asked her what her, father did to her mother. Her father had said to her that " this was what all this drinking, falling, and knocking about would come to."A few weeks before her death her mother had a bad cut on her forehead, and she had to have her heal bandaged. The marks were on her fore- head at the time of her death. She had frequently seen bruises on her mother as a consequence of fiehts in which she had been engaged. When her father was accused of killing his wife he said it was a lie.—Re- examined by Mr. Hope: It was not true that her ' mother was going to the closet and fell downstairs. Wit- ness was asked why she made that statement. but no reply could be elicited from her at first. At leegth she said she did not know why. She told the truth ebout the matter before her father was locked up. 'There was snuff on her mother's face. She was in the habit of tak- ing snuff when she went to Mrs. Connor's. Her mother' did not scream while she was being assaulted, but she said "Oh!oh I" and offered to give prisoner some money for a pint of beer. Mary Jane Fernley, 15, Oliver-ste spoke to taking the prisoner home on the night of the 7th September. She went into the house with him. He said " The house is as I left it at six o'clock ; there has not been a b---- thing done since." 14e then got off the chair,came to the deor, and said "If I catch hold of the b--cow—." She then screamed.—By the Judge : What made you scream ?—He came towards me making a butting mo- tion.—Continuineeshe said she saw the prisoner's wife at 8 30 the same night in company with her motherein-law, Mrs. Bate. The deceased was drunk. Witness saw her tumble on her knee,and then roll over on her back. Wit- ness picked her up.—Cross-examined by Mr.McKeande She had seen the deceased drunk. She didn't' see the deceased's head come in contact with the pavement—By the Judge : If it had done would you have seen it P—l think se. At this point the Court adjourned for luncheon. At two o'clock the C urt re-assembled, and the first witnese called was Victoria Wheelan, widow, 13, .Oliver-street, who said she knew the deceased by sight. They had been her next door neighbours for three weeks. She remembered the September 7th, and on that date witness slept in the hack bedroom of her house. She went to bed a little after eleven o'clock. She was awakened a short time afterwards by the prisoner's child crying out " Dadiy don't lay on coy mammy." The noise continued for about twenty minutes. She heard the deceased say either" 0 Jim you are murdering me ;" or " you have murdered me." He replied with a foul ex- pressien and "Its a laying on yen want." She also heard the deceased say" 11l give you 21el. for a pint of beer." Prisoner did not reply to that but kept on " thunging " her with his hand, and cursing. A few minutes afterwards she heard a jurgling noise. She could hear quite plainly what was going on.—By Mr. M. Keand : She had had no acquaintance with either the deceased or prisoner. It didn't strike her as being time to interfere when it was going on. It was every Saturday, Sunday, and Monday alike. She did attach some importance to it, but it was nothing more than usual. The" thunging "noise was not that of a man walking. If a man were walking about in his stocking feet that would not make the thunging noise. It was more like bumping on the floor. By Mr. McKeand: Then why did you say before the magistrates that the bumps were against the wall ?—lt was against the wall.—Are you prepared to say whether it was against the wall or on the floor ?—lt was against the wall. She never made such a statement to the girl Maggie that her mother would haunt her. She did say to the girl in the presence of Inspector Spinks "'fell the truth, don't tell a lie ; I heard it through the wall," She had not the child alone with her when she put questions to the girl.—Re-examined by Mr. Collingwood Hope, witness said she meant the bumps were near against the wall when she said against the wall. There was no truth whatever in the suggestion that she had the little girl in the house alone with her and asked her questions. Elizabeth Shaw, widow, living with Mrs. Wheelan, her mother, at 13 Oliver-st., gave corroborative evidence as to the sounds and conversation heard whilst she was in bed on the night in question. Mary Ann Clancey, wife of Richard Clancey, a forge- man, living at 9, Oliver-street, stated that she saw the deceased at four o'clock on the 7th September, and also between 7.30 and 8 o'clock. She was drunk the second time she saw her. She went to bed a little after ten o'clock,and heard the prisoner go upstairs with his boots on. He first went into the front room, and afterwards to the back room. She next beard some bad language, and afterwards some children screaming " Daddy don't 'lay on' Mamma." She also heard the deceased say " 0 Jim, you have murdered ;sae," and he replied "Yes, that is what I intend to do." He also said " You'll lay no more of my b— money out."—Cross-examined by Mr. McKeand she said an officer came to her the previ- ous week and asked her to give evidence. The police officer was the first she opened her mouth to. It caused a good deal of excitement in the neighbourhood. She never heard any screams. She didn't think much ot it because it was regular. Deceased was a drunken woman. Richard Clancey, foreman, said on September 7th he was lodging at 9, Oliver-street, he beard a noise in Mr. Bate's. He corrotorated the last witness's evidence.— Cross-examined by Mr. McKeand he said he did mention to a police officer the day after the occurrence that be had heard rows in Bate's house before. Henry Gibbons, labourer, 9, Oliver-street, gave evi- ence as to hearing noises in Bate's house on the night of September 7th. He heard the deceased woman say "Oh!" four times, when the bumping was going on. He could distinguish between the bumping and walking in the room. Ellen Brown,wife of Walter Brown, general iabourer, said she lived at 18,01iver-street. She saw the deceased wcman on September 7th at about a quarter to ten. De- ceased had had a drop of driale. The following morning the girl Maggie brought a message to her, and she went to the deceased's house. The prisoner was in and she asked him where Kate was,and he replied " Oh! Mrs. Brown lamin a bit of trouble." He asked her to help him to remove the body to the next room. The deceased had nothing on but a chemise and that was cut in the frent. The body was cold. Witness and the prisoner removed the body to the front rooti.—Cross-exansined, by Mr. McKeand : She was quite sure as to the amount of clothing on the deceased. The prisoner said he was going to report the matter to the police, and she told him to do so without delay. Margaret Kelly, wife of Patrick Kelly, 30, Foundry- street, general labourer, said the deceased was hey daughter, and was 33 years of age. She had been married to the deceased fourteen years, and had had Paver al children. /They often quarrelled. On September 8, she went to the deceased's house in Oliver-street. She 'saw the prisoner and shouted to him" Jim, what ails Kate." He said "She is dead." Witness then said " Jim you have done it at last ; long threatenings have come at last. You have many a time said you would swing for her." Ile replied that she (witness) was ,a liar; adding that his wife fell downstairs drunk.—Cross- examined by Mr. Mciettni, she said the child Maggie came to her in the afternoon of the Bth September. Her son, John Kelly.wais present. She asked the girl " What happened," aad she replied that her father had killed her mother by kicking her. Sergeant Cheney stated that on Tuesday, the Bth September, he went to the prisoner's house and saw the deceased. He made an examination of the back bedroom. The prisoner was mot there. He arrested the prisoner in 1 Buckley-street the same afternoon, and took him to the Police Station. The prisoner went to kis work but did I not work. At the Police Station he charged the prisoner and he replied "I have to say that I had nothing to do with it at all. Wheu I came home last night,it has been the regular thing for three months for her to be drunk es eryMonday night, and when I entered the house the things were on the table, and the house empty. I came out and went across to Mrs. Brown's and asked one of the girls where Kate was. They told me On was

in Connor's drunk. Iwent there and found her drunk, in company with a lot more women. They were all drunk, and snuff running down their faces. They were drinking beer. I walked° away: I never saw my wife from then till this morning when my daughter woke me about eight o'clock, and asked me to come and look at her mother, as she looked very white. Ii was on the advice of Mrs. Shaw that I came -up Buckley-street to get my mother to accom- pany me to the police station. I was arrested. —Cross-examined by Mr. McKearrd. witness said he had known deceased for about fourteen years. She was a drunken woman. She was frequentlyquarrelsome,and had a good reputation as a fighting woman. Sue had been summoned five or six times at the Borough Court. There might be eight or nine convictions and tome dis- missals.

Detective Inspector Spinks said he visited the de- ceased's house on September ath, at about one o'clock. He examined the stairs and the walls adjoining. The piece of cloth produced was given to him by the prisoner's daughter. The oth..r piece of cloth was cut from the bed, and was saturated with blood. The boats produced ere being worn by the prisoner at the time of his arrest.—Cross-examined by Mr. McKeand, witness had a conversation with the girl in his presence. Before he went into the house•Mrs.Wiaeelan and Mrs. Kelly were with the girl. • Dr. Starkey Smith said on Tuesday, the iith Septem- ber, he made an examination of the body. He examined a piece of hedtick under a microscope, and it was marked with blood. The body was a well-nourished one. On centre of the forehead there was an , extensive bruise, 1 inches in diameter. It was a fresh wound. He did not think one blow would be sufficient to cause the wound The tip of the nose was discolourd-and de- pressed. There, was a contusion on the right side of the chin, and under the left ear there was a discolouration about three inch long. The wound were all. recent. He thought death must have taken place soon after the infliction of the injuries or they would have had other evidence of discolouration. There was a contusion on the right fore arm ; a bruise on the right wrist; and,also on the middle finger. There was also a contusion on the left hip, and four or five on the knee. All these in- juries were of recent date. In company with Dr. Bow- den he made .a post-mortern examination. The cause ,of death was an effusion of blood on the brain caused by direct violence. There was nothing to count for death in the organs of the body. The blow on the forehead Was sufficient to cause death. Death in his opinion followed within an hour or two from, the de- livery of the blows. The injuries h e found were not compatible with the idea of tap ,deceased having. fallen downstairs. He did not think the injuries could' have been caused by falling downstairs. He saw the blood on the door in the back bedroom. • He thought it had come chiefly from the deceased woman's nose. The bruise on the chin looked as if the tip of the boot had caused it,but that on the forehead must have been by the blunt part of the boots, and ,not the tips. This concluded the evidence for the day. Mr. Mclteand at the suggestion of his lordship, postponed his cross-ex- amination of Dr. :Starkey Smitn until the following morning.

SOCIALIST lIRICTING.

Mr. J. Taylor Kay, of Manchester, read in excel- lent paper on "Tee Pollution of Rivers" at &meet- ing of socialists in the Druids' Hall, Sankey-at., on Sunday evening. The lecturer pointed out that the weakness of the early Pollution of Rivers' Act was due to the lack of provisions for co-ordinating I authorities.. After alluding to the evil effects of ' river pollution on the health of the inhabitants liv- ing on the banks of these impure streams, Mr. Kay proceeded to refer to the economical sine of the question, speaking of the loss of cattle and fish to .the community, and the great effect it had on the 'supply of water to townships. lie condemned the proposed sewage scheme; of the Manchester, Cor- poration on the grounds that it would withdraw some of the water from the river, and that it was wasteful in many. ways. Ile contended that it was I quite possible to carry out the sewage scheme without causing waste, inconvenience, or annoy- ance, on lines which had been laid down by emin- ent sanitary engineers.

SKE-TOH-E8 OF NEW ENGLAND LIFE."

At the meeting of the Wycliffe Young People's Society on Monday. evening, Miss Porritt read a paper with the above title, in which she graphic- ally sketched lite as she saw it in the New England states ot America during a recent visit. The early part of the paper was devoted to a description of the , voyage across the Atlantic to Canada, anda short account of her travels through a portion of the dominion on her way to New York. The essayist's description of life in Connecticut—one of the New England States—was intensely intersting. M ela- tion was made of the Puritans' early settlement .iz these states, and contrasts between English and thio: New Englander's life were shown, although in the main, she said, the difference is not very groat. A short discussion ensued, and Miss Porritt was thanked for her paper on the motion of M. Joseph Podmore, seconded by Mr. C. Lyon, and supported by the Chairman (Mr. Pickavance).

THE NATION'S HEALTH.

It is becoming mere and more evident year by year to those who minutely ohserve the current of internal events and especially the health of the British nation that we are fast becoming a nation of Dyspeptics. The malady of dyspepsia has spread its effects not only over our own nation, hut it is the prevailing malady of civi- lised life. There is no disease more prevalent or more difficult to cure. It is a disease of the stomach and as- sumes a variety of different forms in parts of the body far removed from the seat of the disease. Dyepepsia is only another name for the effects of indigestion and the different forms it assumes as its effects are known by various names, according to the different symptons it presents, as dyspepsia flatulence, heartburn, sick head- ache, pains in the stomach, palpitation of the heart, heart diseases,colic, dysentery, diarrheee, &c. All these and even apoplexy and paralysis and nine tenths of the eases of convulsions in children have their source in that , dereetive nutrition to which the term indigestion has be - -Defied. Constant overworking of the stomach must pro- duce disease of that organ. More nutriment is furnished to the tissues of the body by a small quantity of food well digested, than by a large quantity half digested. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

ANOTHER WARRINGTON PUBLICAN PROSECUTED.

On Monday morning at the Warrington Borough Court before the Mayor (Alderman James Fairclough) in the chair. Alderman Harrisnn, Mr. T. B;rchall, and Mr. J. W. Hayward, Michael Higgins, landlord of the Royal Arms. Bank-street,was charged with being drunk on his own licensed premiaos on November 2nd.—Mr. Browne represented the defendant.—The Chief Constable (Mr. L. Talbot) said the defendant had been landlord of the" Ravel Arms" since 27th April. On the 2nd of November. P.C. Metcalfe was requested to come to the "Royal Arms," and on going there he found defendant sitting in the kitchen and in aa almost helpless state of drunkenness. The wife of defendant made a complaint about big conduct. The officer asked defendant to stand up and he attempted to do so. hut tumbled dawn after clutching wildly at the mantelpiece. Metcalfe then went to report the matter to his sergeant and they then returned and found that defendant lad been put to bed. Defendant was seen drunk by the landlord of the " Royal Oak," who sent him home in a cab. They had the man who drove the cab, a customer. Mr Lewis. county court bailiff, and two clerks, who all saw defendant helplessly drunk. If he could not conduct himself he could not conduct a license.—No evidence waa called as Mr. Browne did not dispute the facts; but added in extenua- tion that defendant was a most respectable man. He had been ill and under the rare of Dr. Geary for some- thing like nine weeks. On the dar of the election he went out and had a drink with a number of friends in Bridge-street. He had te go to the brewery on business and coming back he had four glasses of beer, and these in his weakened state took effect upon him. As the owners of the public-house would most likely give defendant notice to quit, he asked the bench to be as lenient as possible with him.—Defendant was discharged or payment of costs.

Newton. and 22 and 24 Mercer-street, Newi on, in contraven- tion of the Public Health Act. anel further with neglecting to , abate the nuisanre when requested to do so by an order of I the Newton Urban District Council. Mr. Shirley. clerk to the Council prosecuted. and stated that the defendant had neglected certain provisions with regard to ashpits. On the 25th of August the defendant was served with an order to abate the nuisance which existed on ha premises. but he had refused to do so. The nuisance Was no only very objectionable but a source of danger to the public health.— John Gregory,assist- ant nuisance inspector.stated that he had visited the defend- ant's premises. A nuisance existed there and *as injurious to health. He served tta defendant with a notice to aba, e it, but when he last visited the premises the work had not been done.—Mr Shirley said he applied for an order of the magis- trates for the abatementof the nuisance within seven days, and for a fine of ss. each day the nuisance existed after the expiration of that time.—The defendant did not appear, but a friend who appeared on his behalf said the work had been done.—The Assistant Nuisance inspector said it was not done when he visited the premises on the previous Wednesday.— The magistrates made an order for the abatement of the nuisance. AN UNREGISTERED LODGING Hot:rim—HEAVY PINE.— Henry Plant, of Eariestown, was charged with keeping an unregistered lodging house at 21, Grafton-street, Eariestown. —The defendant did not appear.—Mr. Shiley, clerk to the Newton Urban District Council, prosecuted, and stated that shortly after midnight on the 16th of October, the Council's Assistant Nuisance Inspector. In company with P.O. Burns visited the house kept by the defendant, and there found seventeen persons, fourteen of whom the defendant admitted were casual lodgers, The offence was the more serious from the fact that the defendant was unable to plead ignorance for oa the 3rd August. the Assistant Nuisance bug actor asked the defendant to send in an application to the Council for a license. But the defendant had set the Council at defiance, and was continuing to keep lodg-rs up to the present time.— John Gregory, assistant nuisance inspector, spoke to visiting the defendant's house. On the date mentioned he found 17 casual lodgers in the house. The assistant inspector then detailedthe overcrowding which ne found, and said the house was in a filthy condition. It was totally unfit for human habitation. He had previously called upon the defendant to ask him to apply to the Council for a license.—P.C. Burns stated that he accompante t the last witness. and corroborated his statement as to the number found in the house.—Mr. Shirley applied for a fine of £5 and a penalty of £1 per day so long as the defendant continued without a lice tse.—The Clerk That is the maximum line.—The Bench imposed a tine of 40s. and costs. HEREDITY.

If you read the articles upon scientific subjects which are published in the reviews, magazines, newspapers, and medical journals of the day, you cannot help being impressed with the attention being given to the inherited tendencies of children. There are, of course, many con dieting opinions ; but upon one point all the writers are agreed—this is in relation to the importance of nutrition during the period of growth. We are furnished with many illustrations to prove that during this growing age is determined, to a great extent, the size and health of the adult body, which in turn influences the next and future generations. For instance, we have it pointed out to us that, in their natural wild state, the size of horses and cattle varies in proportion to the food af- forded them by Natute. In northern countries, where food is searce, we find the Shetland pony, for example ; while in Western America the horses are nvich larger. Let us apply this truth to nur children. How often it happens that, notwithstanding the fondest care, children are utterly unable to assimilato the kind of nourishment that would make them thrive. They make no progress, lave difficulty in forming their teeth, and frequently develop alarming symptoms. A lady in Kent ei.deri- enced something of this difficulty. Her baby had every- thing a b tby would seem to need, and yet its progress was unsatisfactory. The lady herself, in a letter telling about her baby says :

"At six months she was a really beautiful baby, but after she was weaned I was greatly disappointed at the little progress she made She was not in ary way ill, and was a decidedly ge :'-tempered child, bu, made aI.J- solutely no progress, although I devoted my entire time to her. I could not think it right that a child so well taken care of. and Po much in the fresh air, should lose flesh in spite of apparent good health. When she was NAven months old she hal cut four teeth, but in the nee five months cut only L:::e more. At this timf, when baby was one year old, I began giving .ler Scott's Emulsion. I have much - pleasure in saying that she improved in a marve.thns manner. Baby took the Emulon with evident enjoy,nent, and I may say it was just what she needed to give her flesh, strength, and vitality, Yours very truly, (signed) MRS. GEORGE HANDEL, Mayesden, Whitstable, Kent, December 19th, 1895,"

Now the truth is, what the little baby of the foregoin g letter really needed was just what her mother gave her—Scott's Emulsion. If you want to be convinced of what this preparation has done fort cod-liver oil and hypopbosphites of lime and soda, try it on a baby that does not thrive. In the most extreme cases of emacia- tion and wasting, Scott's Emulsion is almost invariably all that is required to restore strength, flesh, vitality, and health. It is of the greatest assistance in forming the growing body, both in giving flesh and forming solid bones, and has a direct bearing upon the early tend- encies of life, to which the scientists are giving such profound attention.

OUR LOCAL CT:LURCHES. CAIRO-STREET CHAPEL.

At this place of w arship on Sunday, the Rev. W. H. Drummond preached the third of a series of sermons on "The early stories of the Bible and their meaning for us," taking on this occasion the sturr of " The F 411." The speaker remarked that, a I)n' time at an earnest thinker stood face to face with a crreat problem—the problem ef the origin of the sin and wickedness and con- sequent misery he saw around him. Others had studied the same question,aml had arrived at various conclusions some of which the preacher outlined, and this thinker set down his idea of the origin of sin, which might be looked upon as an excellent parable of human life.show- ing deep inslaht into human nature, the progress of temptation. Ad the excusee men make for yielding thereto. flis story of "The Fall " was the fundamental basis of the orthodox doctrine of " original sin," yet it was strange that the Bible was, with the exception of one or two rererenees to Adam by St. Paul in his epistles, entirely silent on the matter, and it was not until we descended some hundreds of years in ecclesiastical his- tory that the doctrine was taught with any definiteness. The knowledge we now pogs.d of the history of the human race discredited .the arr‘i-icr of the story re- corded in Genesis, showing that instead of a " fall " from perfection there had been a gradual growth up- ward. But the existence of sin and its inevitable retri- bution even unto subsequent generations was an unclen4- able fact, and the best remedy for Us was the Christi- anity of the gospel.

LATCHFORD BAPTIST CFIURCH.

The third a-r-ni-v-e;s—ary-CA the Christian Endeavour Society was held on Tuesday. The proceedings com- menced with ,a public tea, which was largely attended. After tea, an Interesting entertainment was given in the schoolroom, by various members and friends of the society, the programme consisting of songs, recitations and addresses. The Rev. J. S. Hughes spoke on "Bible Reading," Mr. F. Eves on "A Pure Sunday," and Mr. T. Albinson on "Christian Endeavour Influence." The various reports showed the society to be in a healthy condition. The President (Rev. C. Andrew) presided.

WARRINGTON T. ST. HbLENS.

The above fixture in the Lancashire League came off at St. Helens on Saturday in wet weather. There were not more than 1,000 spectators, and the ground was heavy. Warrington had only a weak team, " Smack " Bate (captain). F. Barber, and three forwards being absentees. St. Helens had their best fifteen on. The combinations were as follows :—Warrington : Heesom, full back; La,wlees, Moores, Cross, and Radford, three- quarters; Houghton and Higginbottom, ' half-backs; Higham, Swift, Taylor, Hill, Jolley, Roby, Scholtze, and Meecock, forwards. St. Helens: Foulkes, full back; Doherty,Jaques, Traynor, and Archer, threequarters ; ' Cross and O'Hara, half-hacks; T. Winstanley, W. Wm- stanley, S. Rimmer, P. Dale, J. Reynolds, W. Briers, W. Whitely, and J. Brownbill, forwards. Mr. Underwood of Wigan was referee.

Cross kicked off for the Saints against a strong wind and Moores returned, Traynor kicking in touch. The homesters wheeled the first scrimmage and Higginbot- tom saved. Foulkes returned a kick, and Houghton by a good punt put the visitors on the offensive. Cross got the oval away and transferred to Traynor, but Doherty failed at his turn. St. Helens were next penalised and Taylor took the kick. Then followed a spell of cross dribbling all over the home half, and the ball coming to Heesom he put it in touch. Houghton got in another useful kick from behind the pack, and the visitors pressed strongly. Thornton looked like scoring when he inadvertently knocked-on. Briers gave the homesters timely relief by running smartly to the Warrington 25. O'Hara made a dash but was met by Houghton, and Fligginbottom relieved temporarily. The next scrim- mage saw O'Flara find touch again in Warrington quar- ters and the Saints gained close proximity to the visitors' line by good dribbling. A couple of good punts by Hig- ginbottom sent the Warringtonians into the home half where Foulkes was tackled. Radford almost let Tray- nor get away but another of Houghton's judicious kicks stopped the movement. Passing along the Warrington backs reached Lawless, who made a good run and War- rington were soon on the Saints line again. Moores at- tempted to drop but the shot was futile,and Cross pulled Traynor down when just getting away. Briers was soon off again with the ball at his toes but the referee ruled him back for off-side. The home forwards then drib- bled into Warrington quarters and Moores relieved with a strong kick. The interval arrived with a black score sheet.

Paylor re-started and Houghton tackled Cross with the ball. A free kick however put the homesters strongly on the offensive and Houghton getting off-side, Warring- ton were penalised and Archer had hard lines with the Penalty kick. Taylor then came through the scrimmage and aided by Houghton gained considerable ground. Another forward dribble by the Saints called upon Ideesont who saved splendidly and Taylor and Lawless transferred play to the home 25. Meecock charged a kick down and Warrington got right in the corner. Then St. Helene dribbled back again and so the game went, each side repeatedly attacking. Moores intercepted a pass meant for Traynor but was tackled quickly. Hig- ginbottotn ran smartly. Cross then got in a lucky kick which sent the ball over the Warrington line. A race ensued and O'Hara was just on the eve of scoring when Lawless sent the ball dead. Warrington were again penalised but the kiek was useless, though Cross punted to the visitors' 25 flag immediately after. Here Efeea.m saved cleverly and found touch. Another of C-oss's passes was snapped up by Moores who made for the line at top speed ; kicking past Foulkes he lonked a certain scorer when that player got in front of him and saved with a strong flying kick. Play then settled down at half-way and Foulkes dropped at goal from midfield, the ball just passing under the bar. The Saints now played up well but the defence was too strong and the game ended in neither side having notched a point.

JUNIOR LEAGUE.

_ WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S Y. WARRINGTON LOCOS.— These teams met on St. Mary's ground, Liverpool-road, on Saturday. for the first time this season. Punctual start was made. From the kick-off the play was near half-way, but the home team again got away, the Loos. clearing immediately. Deakin scored after a good run, and Roughley improved. The visitors got near to the Saints' linos, and from a free kick Carey failed. Ralf-time—St. Mary's, one goal ; Locos. nil. From the re-start Mason's kick was returned by Thomas and after a scrimmage Bennett got away. The Locos. were awarded a free kick for off-side play, but Siddall made his mark, and from an ensuing scrimmage the vis- itors made a determined rush to the Saints lines. The home forwards, however, relieved, and play was under the Locos.' posts, and a smart pass from Deakin to Siddall enabled the latter to score a try. which was not improved. Soon after Roughley kicked a goal from a penalty kick, whilst Bramhall dropped a goal just before time. The match ended in a win for the St. Mary's by three goals ane try (15 points) to nil.

NOTES AND NEWS.

The issue in Spain of the internal loan for the Cuban campaign has aroused a fever of patriotism like the one that burst out in Prussia after Jena. The Spanish like the Prussian ladies are selling their jewellery. They de not give gold for an ron cross, but for paper whose in- trinsic value may not be much greater if Spain's reverses continue. The Bank of Spain has opened the subscrip- tion without any commission. Al the banks in Madrid have followed this example—even the agencies of the Cre lit Lyonnais and ether French banks. Newspapers like the " Imparcial." " Liberal," and others, besides the support of their leading articles, gave the Govern- ment their fourth page for advertisements of the loan. Bishops, deans, and chapters have promised to give the gold and silver plate of their churches if it should prove necessary to crush the '• enemies of Spain,"as the Cuban and Phillippine insurgents are always called, and pre- serve the integrity of the country. People assemble in crowds to subscribe the loan, amid cries of "Viva Es- pana !" "Viva Cuba Espanola The Madrid stock- brokers have undertaken to dispose of four millions sterling of scrip. This is a larger sum frli that city than twenty or fifty times as much would be for London.

Not only ornithologists, bat the general public as well will be interested to hear of the unusually large number of snow buntings that have been arriving in England during the past week. For the beautiful little bird is generally considered to be the harbinger of a severe win- ter, plentiful in severe, and scarcer in mild, seasons. Last winter, which passed with scarcely any frost or snow, brought us comparatively few of these feathered visitors. The winter before that, with its great frost, brought them in large numbers. This season they are arriving in greater numbers than ever, the marsh lands of the eastern and fen counties being full of their twit- tering. Are we to get our skates ready, and protect our water-pipes ?

Respecting Joe Summers—the last of the celebrated postboys—who died recently at Melton, a correspondent relates the following incident :—" An order came one day from Belvoir Castle to the George Hotel, Melton, for four post-horses to be at the Castle next day, to take a travelling carriage thence to Leicester. Early on the following morning Joe and his fellow postbov went to Balvoir, brought the carriage to Melton, changed horses, and went on te Leicester. They had scarcely got back to Melton when another carriage and four drove up, which had to be taken on to Nottingham. When they arrived at Nottingham they found hut one 'boy' at home ;so Joe had to go on with the Nottingham boy to Derby. He got back to Melton next morning, after bumping saddle 126 miles. He told me he slept on his horse nearly the whole distance between Nottingham and Melton.

A new sociAtvito be known as the V..nezuela Colonisa- tion Sociey, is being organised in the United States,with its headquarters in St. Louis, whence early next year it will start for Venezuela. The Government of that country has given a large grant of land to the society for the purpose of starting the colony, and, in addition, it has undertaken to furnish the people sent out with free transport thither, and to maintain them in food for a month after their arrival. The arrangements are be- ing largely made by Mr. W. H. Reynolds, the secretary of the society, who some five years ago started a colony in Costa Rica, which is said to be in a very flourishing condition. It is expected that a start will be made with a couple of hundred men and their wives and families. It is likely that the chief enterprise which will engage them, at first at all events, will be coffee-growing and gold-mining.

A broad-piece was sold in London on Monday for £770, which is said tn be the highest price ever given in an auction-room for a single coin. This particular piece was known as the Juron Medal, from the fact that it was given to the prelate of that name by Charles I. just before the latter was executed. The price which the piece brought by auction in 1854 was £2BO.

Amongst the other little legislative contracts which the Australian colonies so light-heartedly take in hand (says the " St. James's Gazette "), but which the more nervous, and cautious legislators of the old country are very chary in touching, is the abolition of the barmaid. In south Australia her days are numbered, and she automatically ceases to exist on June 30, 1899. Such is the effect of a clause introduced into the Licensed Victuallers' sill in the Parliament of that colony by a member named O'Malley, who succeeded in carrying it in Committee by a majority of six.

Apples—whether fresh or nreserved—have a fine tonic effect on the stomach ; one good apple usually giving a good appetite in about ten minutes. Eat, therefore,two or three good-sizad apples at every meal. By using the fruit largely, one's physical power of endurance under labour, either mental or physical, are very much in- creased, and flesh is gained. This may be attributed chiefly to the fact that apples assist the digestion and as- similation of foods of other kinds. Besides, chemists r,•- cord that apples contaira a larger percentage of nitrates and phosphates (food for brain and muscle) than any other Luit.

Prince Ferdinand, of Bulgaria, is taking care tha.t his son and heir shall be duly instructed in the new faith to which he has hoer" " converted." The little prince is now deemed to be old enough to attend divine service, and so a chapel has been provided for him, with apart- ments for his religious instructor. The archimanchite Vassili has been appointed to that office, and the neophyte is being regularly initiated into the doctrine and ritual of the Orthodox Church.

THE MA.YOR AND CORPORATION

AT CHURCH.

At the invitation of the nayor the members of te Corporation, officials, and others accompanied him to the Parish Church on Sunday morning. A procession was formed in front of the Town Hall at ten o'clock, headed by the Borough Brass Band playing appropriate :nusic. Then followed 53 members or the borough police force under the direction of the Chief Constable, and the firemen under the superintende.nce of Mr. Turner. All presented a very smart appearance. Tiae Mayor (Alderman James Fairciough) had the+ Town Clerk on his right and his chaplain (the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton) on his left. The members of the Corporatios present were: Aldermen Franeomb, Wright, and Flutchinon. and Councillors Rob ,rts, Heaton, Shaw Green, Dr. Ca nnell, R. W. Monks, Smethurst, Bennett, Shillcock, Baird, Evans, Pierpoint, Lee, Rogers, Furness, Tinnion, Jolley, Burton and Grace. The officials present were, in addition to the Town Clerk, Mr. Stanley Wil- son (assistant town clerk), Mr. John Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr. John Sutcliffe (ass;stant borough sur- veyor), Mr. George F. F. White (deputy clerk to the magistrates), Mr. Robert Young (cemetery registrar), Mr. W. S. Howard (cashier of the Gas Department),Mr. Charles Madeley (librarian, curator, So). The rear was brought up by Messrs. Thomas May, Thomas Tunstall, William Lawless. Samuel Hunt, Hugh Fairhurst,Thomas Birtles, Genrie Watkins, H. Toft, W. J. Shaw, E. Auk- land, and T. Morris..

. - On arrival at the church gates,the Mayor and members of the Corporation were met by the Iletor (the Rev. Canon Willis), the Rev. G. B. Thurston, and the Rev. E. J. Wil!cocks, who preceded them into the builling. Seats were reserved for those in the procession. There was a large congregation. The prayers were read by the Rector and the Rev. G. B. Thurston, and the lessons by the Rev. E. J. Willcocks. Mr. W. S. Woods was at the organ. The sermou was preached by the Rev. E C E. Carleton from the words : '• Who went about doing good," Acts x. part of the 38th verse. The preacher re- marked that it would seem that in these days the teach- ings a d the researches of science w?re bringing them nearer and nearer to the doctrines of divine revelation. Certainly modern scientific research would seem to be apnroaching more and more that miraculous realm which we s supposed to be religion's special province, and which for a long time it refused to recognize. He wiebed to enforce the fact that Jesus did gool, although he should be colt° willing to leave the doctrine to be tried by thet test which modern scientists were else eys ready to accept as a proof of excellence—he meant the test of" the survival of the fittest," the survival of the religion of Christ over all philosophy and speculation. The oracles of Greece were dumb,Rnme's costliest shrines were crumbling to dust, but the religion founded by the peasants of Galilee under the Saviour's inspiration still commanded, and had commanded in all ages, the horn- wee and the allegiance el the most civilized nations of the world. Christ's work was specific rather than general, and that was the kind of work which, as a rule, had very little attraction for them. Christ thought less of the crowd and more of the individual. He signalled out men with a special discern- ment, interest and sympathy, and love ; He narrowed the area of His work, but to that extent He gave it depth. He kept in mind the needs of body and soul. Men were to a great extent creating their own needs, develoning in themselves an unhealthy appetite which grew the more it was satisfied, and as an abnormal appetite for food might be a symptom of disease in the body so disease in the soul might show itself by a simile! creation. The preacher, in conclusion, showed that Christ's vrotk wag not only disinterested, hut that it involved sacrifices, as all good work did. If .eey woul J do any good in the world they must pay the penal.e. They would make enemies. They woeld be misunder- stood, and it might be disliked, and the really vicious would stir up hatred against them falsely ; but the good work remained, that could not perish.
