WARRINGTON BRITISH WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the above assl3iation was held in the Bold-street schoolroom on Wednesday evening. There was a fairly good attendance and the chair was taken by Miss Be Dutton. There were also on the platform Mrs. John Yong, Mrs. Gunnell, (secretary), Mrs. Braysha.w (Manchester), and Mrs. Garnett.. Mrs. Roberts, (treasurer) and Miss Bradbury (joint secretary) were also present.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
SEC RETA RY'S RETORT.

After devotional exercises the following report was read by the secretary, Mrs. Gunnell : Is presenting their report for another year the commit- tee feel that their progress has been more in the direc- tion of consolidation than of extension. Still they trust that this root growth will result in larger and more abundant fruit in future months and years. Upwards of seventy pledges have bsen taken during the year, though from various causes the number of public meet- ings has no been so large as in previous years. The annual meeting was held in the Lecture Hall of the Y.M C.A. on October 31st, 1695, when Mrs. Green, of Fazakerley, Liverpool, gave an interesting account of temperance work among working women in Liverpool, St. Helens, &c. Meetings were afterwards held at Cock- hedge Mission Room,Moore,and Sankey Bridges. Several new pledges were taken at Moore, and at the Sankey Bridges meeting a petition was numerously signed in favour of Mr. Stevenson's Sunday Closing Bill, which was at that time before the House of Commons. Most of the speeches were on that particular phase of the tem- perance question. On March 11th, 1596, we were favoured with a visit from Miss Agnes Slack, honorary secretary of the B.W.T.A. The meeting which was held in the Bold-street Wesleyan Lecture Hall, was more largely attended than any we have previously held in Warrington, and vas ably pre- sided over by Mrs. John Fairclorigh. A large number signed the pledge in response to Miss Slack's appeal, and 16 new mrmbers were added to our ranks, The Com- mittee look forward hopefully to another year of prayer- ful and diligent effort. They note with thankfulness, a deepening and widening horror of the evils wrought by the drink; a sure and steady growth of temperance sentiment ; a deeper sense of personal responsibility among Christian women ; and a greater willingness to practice self-denial, if need be, for the sake of those " ready to perish " for whom Christ died. For these , "signs of the times" we thank God, and under their inspiring influence, we take courage for future toil. The officers for the ensuing year were—president, Mrs. Yonge ; vice-presidents, Mrs. Garnett and Miss Dutton ; committee. Mrs. Reed, Mrs. Potts, Mrs. John Fairclough, Mrs. J. B. Isaac, Miss Green and Miss Vanda Taylor ; treasurer, Mrs. H. Roberts ; secretaries, Miss Gunnell and Miss Bradbury.

A letter of apology for non-attendance was read from Mrs. John Fair( lough.

TREASURER'S REPORT.

Mrs. GUNNELL then read the report of the treasurer which showed that they commenced the year with a balance in hand of £2 12s. 91. Subscriptions and col- lections realised £9 13s. 911., making a total of £lO 6s. Bd. The expenses for printing, posting, hire of hall, &c., amounted to £7 17s. 80. The balance in hand was thus £2 83. 10±i. Miss DUTTON said they could not be ashamed of the report, though they wanted more enthnsiasm and a more frequent recurrence of meetings. (Applause.) Mrs. BRAYSHkW, in the course of an interesting address en the evils of drink, said all of them had influence over someone or other and they must remember that they were to some extent their brother's keeper, because the law of nature was that we lived in families and com- munities and no one of us could do an action that had not some influence over someone else. Referring to the hereditary tendency to drunkenness, the speaker said it was a curious thing and we did not know how far back some of our ancestors might have indulged in strong drink, the liking for which might crop up generations after. The safest thing was to touch not, taste not, and handle not. If we never took any drink we could never become drundards. It was the moderate drinkers who made the drunkards, for no one began with being a drunkard. They all began with being moderate drinkers, hoping that they would be able to control their appetite, but many found that it was im- possible on continue with moderate drinking. So long as drink-shops were to be found at the corner of every street the poor people were bound to take drink. Their will-power in many cases was utterly gone and then there was the hereditary taste. She wished those who were mothers would remember to bring their children up so that they would neither know the taste or smell of strong drink. In the year 1890 there were 500 children under ten years of age taken into custody in the streets of London for being drunk and incapable. Those 500 children were criminals manufactured by the drink traffic. Mrs. Brayshaw thought it was a reasonable thing for the inhabitants of any neighbourhood to band them- selves together and say they would not have drinkshops in their midst, and she concluded by exhorting all her hearers to do something for the cause. (Applause) Miss DUTTON thanked Mrs. Brayshaw heartily for hr address and for the suggestions she had Diadf..

Mrs. BRAYSHAW moved that the report read by the secretary be adopted. Mrs. JOHN YoNem seconded, and complimented Mrs. Gunnell on the nice report she prepared every year. She then spoke at some length on the work there was to be done in the poorer districts. Meetings should be held there, and Mrs. Yonge asked fot assistance in starting such meetin.s. Referring to the evils of drink and the need for active intervention she said the matron of the Warrington Workhouse gave her testimony to the effscts of drunkenness. Mrs. Ward had said to the speaker that if they took away all the inmates who were there through drink they would have none left. I Miss DUTTON alluding to the vein of sadness which at- tached to the meeting by the loss of Mrs. Parker, said they could only be grateful for the memory of her useful work in life. It would be unfit th At the meeting should not make som ,eoenition of her wr‘rlr. and remember that she was the founder of the great Society they reprc- seeted that night. Mrs. R. GARNETT related the early conversion of Mrs. Parker to temperance. The deceased lady, she said, went to America in the time of the great whisky crusade when the women came out so strongly ; and when she ceme back to Eneland she personally invited a number of fidies from America and the tint great meeting was field. The RrisL Women's Temperance Association was then founded and Mrs. Parker was really the foundress ef it. Since Mr,. Parker died many beautiful testimon- ies had been given to her memory, and Mrs Garnett r( ad a letter from alady.testifying to Mrs. Parker's w, rk twenty years ago. She (Mrs. Garnett) trusted that the occasion might be one of deeper consecration to some of them and that upon them Mrs. Parker's mantle might fall.

Miss DurroN gave an earnest address in which she expressed the hope that the young would take up tem- perance work. She hoped they would have volunteers that night, and that God would surely rain blessing upon them and give them his deepest bliss. (Ap- plause.) The report was then unanimously adopted. During the evening Mrs. F. R. Roberts recited " Shelter," (an American story), and "The Goblins." Both were expressively given and worthy of the appre- ciation they met with. airs. H. M. Ashton accompanied the hymns en the piano. A collection was taken in aid of Lady Henry Somer- set's Armenians Relief Fund. The meeting closed with the benediction pronounced by Miss Dutton.

PERFECT THRIFT BUILDING SOCIETY

A special meeting of the members of the War- rington and District Perfect Thrift Building Society was held in the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. It was called to consider the ad- visability of balloting the full number of shares held by each member instead of one share only as had previously been the case. After discussion, it was unanimously decided that in fature the ballot should be for the full number of shares held by each member.

GENERAL BOOTH AT THE

===== 0002601_18961121_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
PARR HA.T.L.

MEETING LAST NIGHT.

On Friday nigh t,the leader of the Salvation Army —Geueral±Booth—visited Warrington and addressed a crowded meeting in the Parr Hall. The chair was occupied by Mr. R. Garnett, J.P., and he was sup- ported on the platform by General Booth, Colonel Lawley (London), Colonel Taylor (London), Captain Russell (Warrington), Brigadier Howe (Liverpool), Major Scott (London), Adjutant Pepol (Liverpool), Revs. John Yonge, W. 11, Orummond, W. Potts, G. Harbottle, C. Finch, E. T. Reed; Councillors H. Roberts, J.P., William Pierpoint, J.P., Thomas Burton, Captain Rodger, Dr. T. S. Smith, Messrs. C. E. Parker, Thomas Morris, R. E. Garnett, R. G. Henshall, Isaac Hayward, R. 11. Posnett (Runcorn), Bailey, S. B. Stringer, and many others. The CHAIRMAN said he had received a number of replies to the invitations to that meeting which he had sent out. He did not intend to read those letters. He had one from Canon Willis who sympathised with every good work as they might be sure;there were also letters from the Revs. Mr. Carleton and Mr. Percival,Mr. John Crosfield, Mr. Arthur Crosfield who would have been there but for a severe attack of influenza, from Mr. Guest,Mr. Armitage,and many others,all more or less in sympathy with the glorious work in which General Booth was engaged. (Hear, hear.) There was, however' one letter which he had received that he would read. This letter was from Mr. Henry Thornton and expressed sym- pathy with che object of the meeting, and in it was en- closed a cheque ot the value of £5O to be handed over to General Booth. (Loud applause.) That letter had put him (the Chairman) in good spirits as it gave him the feeling that they had every prospect of a good meet- ing both financially and otherwise. (Hear,hear.) There was no feature of General Bloth's work that he had felt mcre deep interest in than the work in connection with the social scheme. He thought their religious com- munities had been a long time in learning that men had bodies as well as souls. The publication of General Booth': book "Darkest England" had done more than any other work published in recent years to bring the whole question of the submereed before the reading public. (Hear, hear.) There were many branches of social work in connection with that movement which he thought most commend themselves to every Christian mind. (Heat hear, and applause). General BOOTH was received with loud applause. He mentioned that that was his eighteenth heavy meeting since last Saturday night and he was sorry to say that he had slightly broken down during tne day time. He was, he said, expected to speak on the social operations of the Salvation Army, but he rather dissented from the distinction that was being made between the spiritual and the secular. They had from the beginning put their arms around men,tody and soul, and tried to bless them in every relationship and circumstance of their lives. He and these who laboured with him had seen the most marvellous transformation of character and cir- cumstances effected by the simple belief in the gospel of Jesus Christ. He did not come there in any spirit of self-glorification although a great work had been done, nor did he come in any spirit of depre- ciation of other platforms of philanthropy and Chris- tian labour, though of course he thought his organisa- tion was at the top of the tree. (Laughter and applause.) He referred to the origin of the Salvation Army thirty-one years ago, and he said that he thought its history was an encouragement to all who wanted to do something for their generation. (Hear, hear.) They began in the east of London, and had spread throughout the country into foreign countries, and he now some- times told his geographical friends that if they did not make haste he would get to the North Pole first. (Laugh- ter and applause.) In India they had hundreds of officers and they had there made a beginning with one of the most marvellous successes in missionary enterprise that the world had ever seen. (Applause.) The flag of the Army was floating in forty-five different countries and colonies and all this had been done in thirty-one years which was not a long period in the history of an ecclesiastical organisation. (Applause.) Six years ago he turned his attention to the temporal miseries of mankind. (Applause.) He went and uncovered the deeper recesses in which vice and crime were huddled together. (Applause.) The methods by which he in- tended to remove these social miseries he published in " Darkest England, and the Way Out." From that time to the present he and his people had been endeavouring to work out the plans that were described in the book. So far they had only been able to deal with the sedi- ment—the bottoms—the lower strata of society. The principles upon which they acted and the methods that they had adopted were adapted to meet the needs of all classes of society but up to the present their time had been taken up and the little money placed at their com- mand had been spent in dealing with the condition of the most wretched of the wretched. (Applause.) He then proceeded to speak of the condition of the people that were rescued by his officers, who had been called the "slum angels." There were some people who told him that he could not lift up these people. But couldn't he ? (Applause.) He had heard men say "We don't want any more blacklegs, we have enough of them. Don't bring any of these men up; we have enough." He replied to that,that they must convert the blacklegs into whitelegs. (Applause.) They must not only make them producers but make them consumers—(applause)—and if they could not find employment for them here, he would remove thew to other fields. (Applause.) Pro- ceeding to refer to the causes of the condition of these wretched people, he spoke of the iniquity of pub- lic-houses, and said they could do with sending the publicans on furlough for three or four years—(laughter) —or thy might go to Turkey fora little time. (Laugh- ter.) But there were people who did evil besides the publican. Thousands and thousands of girls, for the gratification of the appetites of men, had been pulled down from purity, integrity, and comfort, and then thrown upon the streets to bo trampled under the feet of men, and to end their days in the hospital or workhouse. (Shame.) The community ought to very seriously look into this business. Was it not time the Government did something for these women? Continuing the General spoke of the changed nature of the people who came under the charge of his officers and the great success of his system. He confessed that he oould not understand the Chris-ianity of a great many people. There was no topic in the world upon which men wriggled about so much as they did about religion. (Laughter.) If they intended to rescue the lapsed masses and cure the social evils of the present day they must go and seek them. (Applause.) That was what his officers had done, and thee passed the rescued through their " elevators" and made them useful members of so- ciety (Applause). They had had to train their men for this work. It was one thing to have money and the means to do it, and another to get the right men and women to work it. Already they had 1200 officersengaged in this work. It was intended to raise up a new army of crusaders to be called the Red Cross Brigade who would not only pick up the wounded on the battle field, but in all places where they could be found. The speaker then referred to what had already been done by this trigade amongst the Armenians, and the great blessing it had been to that distressed people. In conclusion General Booth asked for the liberal assistance of the audience. They would, he said, be no worse for it in this world and he was certain they would be better for it in the next. (Hear' hear and applause.) Colonel LAWLEY read tha following contributions which had been received :—£s from the Chairman, Mr. R. Garnett; Dr. T. S. Smith, £1; Mr. and Mrs. Wigfield, one guinea ; Mr. A. H.Crosfield, three guineas • Mr. Charles E. Parker, £lO ; and Mr. H. Thornton, £5O:

A collection was then t.iken from the audience. Votes of thanks to the Chairman proposed by General Booth and to the latter proposed by the Chairman were carried unanimously. The meeting then closed with a benediction by General Booth.

Letter tcr tin Miter.

LIBERALS AND IHE ARMENIAN QUESTION.

Sir,—Permit me to inform yours readers that a private conference of Liberal politicians from London arid the provinces was held on Wednesday, November 11th, at the Inns of Court Hotel, when the following resolutions were passed :—I. This meeting is of opinion that the policy of non-party agitation in relation to the Armenian Question has been a failure, and that the Question should be taken up by the Liberal Party on party lines. 2. ,This meeting recognises that the present Eastern sitnation is mainly the result of the Conservative Policy in the past ; that "Lord Salisbury, a . a Member of the Berlin Congress, is especially responsible for the existing t-'l"6 of affairs in Armenia ; and, therefore, that the, pr-lent Ministry cannot be trusted to deal with the Arc:. ,flian Question. An Executive Committee was appc,, .ed te give effect to the foregoing resolutions. It is pr0m.,..,;•,1 to organize Party Meetings throughout the country. Everyone who is willing to co-operate is in- vited to write "to us at the undermentioned address.— Your Obedient Servants, GEORGE W. F. RUSSELL (Chairman.) P. W. CLAYDEN (Secretary.) H. J. TORR (Treasurer.) 18, Wilton Street, London, S. W.

THE MARRIAGE LAWS.

Sir,—l notice another letter by Sursum Corda. He does not seem to realise that in too much argument the truth is often lost. I have never stated or thought tnat people ought not to marry or regarded as etnerwise than holy the marriage vow,but when that vow has been de- secrated it is no longer sacred,and I have argued against the injustice of the marriage laws. It a husband violates the seventh commandment and is not guilty of legal • cruelty his wife cannot obtain a divorce, but if a woman violates that commandment and is not guilty of any other misconduct her husband can obtain a divorce and he would be a fool if he did not do so. Men are not suffi- ciently punished or censured for the wrongs they do to women and to society, for sometimes when the existing laws cannot touch them their conduct iS so immoral that they ought to be banished from civilised society. We say that woman is the weaker vessel,vet for certain sins her punishment is incomparably more than man's. Women have had but little influence in making the mar- riage laws,and they are very unjust both to men and women,but as people become more enlightened and less prejudiced the influence of justice will bring about at a time Cdat is not far distant,an alb-ration in these fright- ful laws.—Yours, &c.,

===== 0002601_18961121_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
S. KENYON. [This correspondence is now closed.—Eo.]

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Slr,—Year by year as the cencert seacon cmes round, well-wishers of our locil Musical society ask themselves how it is that the efforts of the Society receive such very poor encouragement from the Warrington public, and many are the reasons given for this unfortunate state of affairs. In the first place, it is the opinion of very many that the prices of admission to the concerts require re- vision, that the prices asked for reserved gallery seats at the Parr Hall are too high, and that the back gallery should be thrown open to the shilling ticketholders, as well as the back of the hall on the ground floor. I feel sure that some such re-arrangement might be undertaken with advantage. Then again it is frequently stated that our proximity to Manch,'ster and Liverpool where one can attend first class concerts at very small charges (even when railway fare is taken into con ideration) Militates considerably against the success of high class concerts here. A reduction in prices at Warrington would diminish considerably the force of this argument, and the increased patronage that the Society might reasonably expect from such reduction should soon jus- tify their action. These are matters which merit the careful consideration of the Musical Society's Committee. But there is another aspect of the affair. Can we call Warrington a musical town, or are we to confess that somewhat stale humour and very indifferent vocalism is all that is required or desired by the majority of our concert-goers,and that perhaps the Musical Society would meet with a larger measure of success if they could see their was to engage a few cheap humorists to enliven (2) the oratorie performances at certain intervals ! One hardly likes to think however, that masterpieces of Han- del sr Mendelssohn have so little attraction, even when excellently interpreted by such capable performers, both vocal and instrumental, as our society possesses. Ido not, however, believe that good music is so entirely un- appreciated amongst us ; it is our all-powerful desire to get our entertainments cheap (or if possible for nothing) that gets the better of our appreciation! There are many hundreds in Warrington who could well afford to pay their 2s. and 3s. for their evening's entertainment who are too close-fisted to invest in anything more expensive than a sixpenny seat at a Saturday concert, and there afe many others, the self-appointed aristocracy of our town, who of course must occupy the best and most prominent positions who would be horrified at the idea of paying more than one shilling for such positions t I believe these are a few of the reasons why the Warring- ton Musical Society does not get that amount of patron- age it well deservee—a patronage which in many a smaller and poorer town such praiseworthy efforts would atways command.—Yours faithfully, SUBSCRIBER.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PARR HAIL CONCERTS.

Again the elements were leagued against the Saturday night concert in Parr Hall. Warrington, hut notwith- standing this drawback there was a fairly large attend- ance. The programme promised beUer things than that of the week before, and the concert eventually proved a ,most entertaining one. No less than seven artistes tent( part, and they were—Soprano, Miss Lizzie Skeet ; eentralto, Miss Emilie Monk; tenor, Mr. E. Edwards ; baritone, Mr. Eaton Batty ; violinist, Mr. T. Boothroyd (Wairington) ' • accompanist, &c., Mr. W. A. ; and hamnrist, Mr. Nelson Jackson. All performed well, ,but the feur who earned the greatest distinction were Miss Skeet, Mr. Nelson Jackson, Mr. Eaton Batty, and Mr.E. Edwards. One evening last season Miss Skeet made her- self quite a favourite with her audience by the splendid service she rendered, when there were more than ()raj- dary calls upon her abilities. She was equally well liked on Saturday, and her first song "The Moorish Maid " called for an eneore. Mise Skeet responded with the comically 'pretty song" The Zuvder Zee." Her next'appearance was to interpret "The lioly City," and in the rendition of this beautiful piece the singer was heard at her 'best. As a soprano she possesses a very clear, flexible, and sweetly-toned voice which again sounded to advantage in " Killarney," the encore demanded for Miss Skeet's previous song. The tenor. Mr. Edwards, found instant favour with " The Ole Umbrella," a darkie-ditty With a swinging air; it was well sung and greatly enhanced by a chorus of male voices which blended harmoniously w;th the repeat lines. The audience exacted a repetition-of the last verse and Mr. Edwards' second song" I don't care," was of similar eitaracter and was also well received. Both by face and voice Mr. Edwards is well adapted for the sinc- ing of plantation songs, which have to be always well done to appear other than silly. The hulk of the work of entertaining fell upon the shoulders of Mr. Nelson Jackson. On four different oc- casions he kept the audience in a state of merriment,his best pieces- being "Drawing Room. Diverters." " Trifiets," and "The Crossing Sweeper." The two former were essentially humorous and eccentric, while the latter (one•of Cornet, Grain's productions) parteok more of the pathetic. It received skilled treatment from Mr. Jackson and was deservedly applauded. In" Tr-- flats," wan included a song entitled "Sandy M'Clusky," constructed as the author said to rhyme with "whuskv." There was much" whusky "in it,and with a fbag-pipe accompaniment on the piano it went down well.' Mr. Eaton Batty'e powerful baritone voice was well-suited to the Parr Hail. He first sang "The Desert," ha a vigorous and, finished manner, and afterwards gave "Comrades to Arms." Both were encored and Mr. Batty responded to the first. He is undoubtedly a very .capable singer and knows well how to use a voice of more than ordinary range. Mr. Tom BoothrOyd's. selectioux on the violin " \raise de Concert," and " Berceuse de Jocelyn." were skilfully executed. An encore was demanded for each. Mr. Boothroyd gives promise of developing into an ex- pert violinist and more than came up to expectations on Saturday evening. The two songs "My Irish Lad," and "For all Eternity." entrusted to Miss Emilie Monk, were treated with taste and skill. Miss Monk's voice, however, is not a contralto of very fine timbre, though her singing generally was good. She was called upon to bow her acknowledgments. A madrigal in which all four vocalists took part, chosen from the Mikado. was One of the most attractive items.on the prnerarnmeoand as a piece of combined harmony preyed a distinct success. Mr. Hollis accompanied efficiently throughout the even- ing.

ROYAL COURT THRITRE.

Professor E. I. Crocker with his troupe of thirty educated horses is paying a return visit to Warring- ton,and during the past week there have been crowded audiences at the Royal Court Theatre, to witness the performances of these • wonderful animals. Professor orocke7 shows the horses as scholars on the way to school, at play, fetching articles in obedience to orders. Then comes the jumping of barred gates, a brilliant tableaux, and horses shown as bellringers. "La Carous.lle " was an original act invented and produced for the first time by Professor Crocker, and was effectively executed by fifteen ponies. There is a court scene which is most laughable; a night scene in camp, and miraculous military drill. The programme is brought to a close by an al fresco battle with horses as contestants. Every item of the programme is performed in a manner which leaves nothing to be desired. The obedience of the animals to the voice of Professor Crocker gives one an insight into the capabilities of dumb animals in this direction. The programme is both pleasing and instructive. A fine string band is also in attendance, under the conductorship of Mr. W. Pond.

SAD DROWNING FATALITY AT 1

RUNCORN.

The body of Mrs. Annie Carroll, of 25 Fox-st., Run- corn, who mysteriously disappeared from her home on Thursday night last week, as reported in our last issue, was found in the Bridgewater Canal Top Locks on Fri- dar night. On Monday Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, held an in- quest on the body at the Police Station, Runcorn.—John Carroll, the husband, deposed that his wife was con- fined on the 17th ult. lie stopped at home three weeke to take care of her. On Thursday morning he started work again, and never saw his wife again alive. His wife never said anything to him to show that she was despondent. Deceased casually earns to meet him at the canal when he would be finishing his work. On Thursday when he left the house he left his mother in -charge oi deceasod.—Sarah Carroll, methez-in-law of deceased, said she had been in attendance on the de- ceased for the last month. She did not appear to be low- spirited at all. Deceased never said to her that she would take away her life. On Thursday about five o'clock the witness and deceased were having tea, when the latter wished to have an apple. Witness in order Co get an apple left the house. On her return sin found , the deceased had gone.—Dr. Robinson said he attended the deceased about the 20th September for a skin infec- tion. Ho did not see anything of her until a few days after her confinement. The last time he saw her was about 4 o'clock on Thursday, when he noticed a great alteration in her manner. She was sitting up dressed, and nearly better, but there was a marked alteration in her manner. Previously, there was nothing in her mental state to indicate the slightest frailty whatever. She seemed quite happy and cheerful.—Elenry Taylor, Batman, 20 Okell-street, said at about nine o'clock on Thursday night he left his home for his flat at Top-locks. I man gave him some boots, which he had picked up out of the water. Witness asked if he had dragged the canal and he said not. He heard of the deceased having left home the same night, and he commenced te grapple for her. He was successful in his second attempt. He gave information to the police and had the body re- moved.—P.C. Gregory said he received information from the last witness. There were no marks of violence on the body. The flats were near to the bank, and ropes were upon the tow path. She might have been tripped into the canal by these ropes.—The coroner hav- ing summed up, the jury returned a verdict of " Found drowned."

===== 0002601_18961121_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFERENCE OF TEACHERS Al

WIDNES.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

On Saturday afternoon a conference of teachers and others interested in education, under the auspices of the Runcorn and Widnes Teachers' Association of the National Union of Teachers, was held in St. Paul's Paro- chial Rooms, WidnEs. There was a large attendance. It was announced that Alderman Wareing, mayor of Widnes, had promised to preside, and a draft of the resolutions had been sent to him,but acting on the advice of the Town Clerk that the resolutions were of a party character, he declined to take the chair. In his absence Mr. F. Neil, chairman of the School Management Com- mittee of the School Board presided. Dr. Beach, of Macclesfield, a membe_ of the executive of the N.U.T., delivered an address en the subject of the "New Education Bill: What it ought to be and what it ought not to be." Alluding to the Mayor's refusal to preside, he said the union would not allow its organiza- tion or affairs to be prostituted for mere party or de- nominational purposes, and he urged that education ought to be outside the arena of party conflict. In any new Bill must be provided further aid for the schools, superannuation of the teachers, reasonable security of tenure for the teacher, the raising of the age limit, and the reform or utter abolition of the Science and Art De- partment. The Very Rev. Dean Finegan (Catholic) proposed a resolution urging the Government to propose such legis- lation as would give to schools working under equal conditions as to educational results equal financial sup- port from public sources. The Rev. Canon Wood seconded the proposition,saying he did so with the greater pleasure because it did not attempt to solve the difficulty which lay in the practical method of carrying out t'le resolution. He did not sup- pose that they would disagree that the money required for the highest and best education of all' classes should be provided by the-State. As to how it was to be done everybody had a different opinion. He had been more or less behind the scenes of what had been go!ng on lately, and he could assure them that the question was an excessively thorny one, and became more se every day. He trusted a solution would be found,but he was not prepared with one at that moment. An animated discussion ensiled.

Sir John Brunner, M.P., proposed as an amendment that the resolution should read, "That this meeting urges the Government to promote such legislation as shall give equal financial support from public sources to all classes rendering equal services to the public, includ- ing equal kindness to all children attending the schools." In alluding to the Mayor's objection to preside, he said he never heard of a Mayor of a borough being pledged to the resolutions submitted at a meeting because he took the chair. When a gentleman did so in his caOacity he was there as the representative of order and fair play. He (Sir John) hoped it would not be understood that in attending the meeting he pledged himself to all that might be saik (Laughter.) He recognised to the full the breadth and liberality of the remarks uttered by Dr. Beach, and he endorsed his statements that the begin- ning and end of all education should be the good of the child. His sympathies as a Nonconformist, were with the 7,000 or 8,000 parishes in this country where there was but one school,and that almost invariably a Church school. He had had his sympathies aroused for parents in such places for many years past, and Ise was glad Dr. Beach also sympathised for those in isolated villages. Recently he had- had an extremely interesting corres- pondence with the Bishop of Chester, and he was glad to announce that his Lordship had been as emphatic and clear as he could wish in the declaration that parents should have the same choice of religious teach- ing in small parishes as in larger areas. He thoroughly agreed with Dr. Beach that teachers should be more independent than they were to-day. It was necessary for the good of the community that teachers should be free from the liability of dismissal for small and insignificant reasons. The matter of tender- ness towards the religious feelings of Noneonform,sta was one very close to his heart. He gave some exam- ples of how Nonconformist children in Church schools had been treated by•clergymen, and said he had person- ally protested against,such conduct ha several instances. He certainly would meet all teachers to be treated with equal tenderness,and he prophesied that in two years to come managers of schools receiving public money, end many of them willingly, would be compelled zo give a share of the control of tho school to their neighbours. The'Rev. Gibeoß Smith,rector of Halewood, seconded the amendment, saying indiscriminate kindness should be shown to children. Dean Finegan and Canon Wood accepted the amend ment.

The Rev. E. Austin (Congregationalist) said if there was equal aid given to Denominational and Roard schools there ought to be equal privileges and control, and he opposad the motion.

The Re;‘;. T. Brackenbury— proposed to amend the reso- lution by adding the words and shall be equally under local and public control.". _ _ _

- This was seconded, and defeated by a large majority, the amended resolution being passed. Mr. Dodd proposed a resobition condemning any prn- posal to resuscitate Clause 27 of the late Education as being calculated to arouse heated controversy with- out affording a satisfactory solution of the religious difficulty: On the suggestion of the Rev. Wright Williams, vicar of Farnworth, the resolution was amended by leaving out the latter portion, and passed. The following resolution was unanimously adoptPd:— " That, oonsidgring the pressure of foreign competition, this meeting deplores any atte-npt to confine educational expendit;ure.within arbitrary limits, and furtlAr asserts that the cost of education should be decided by the benefit resulting to the child and the nation at large."

THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 21, 1896

WARRINGTON COUNTY GU RT.

THIIRSDAY.—Before His Honour Judge Wynne Foulkes. A DISPETID SIGNATURH.—STRANGE CASH.—Mesers. Walton and Co., of Newton, claimed £ll 19s. ad. for goods alleged to have been delivered to Mr. Jae,ob Brookes, plumber, Farnworth, near Bolton.—The latter denied all knowledge of the affair.—The plaintiffs were represented by Mr. R. -Carruthers, Liverpool, and the defendants by Mr. J. P. Monks, Bolton.—Mr. H. Kirk, a traveller in the employ of Messrs. Walton., said he saw the defendant during the Wakes-week at Farnworth in a public-house on the 27th September.—lily Mr. Menks : No one was in the room when the order was transacted. It was not his way of doing business.—Mr. Monks said his client knew nothing whatever about it. He never saw Kirk laefore and they disputed the signature on the order. He also pointed out that the 27th of Srpterrfber was a Sunday and therefore Kirk could not have busi- ness with Mr. Brookes then. The man Kirk did not know the name of the public-house at which he received the order, or the name of the street.—Henry Brookes, son of defendant, on being handed the order book with his father's name appended denied that it was his father's signature.—Jacob Brookes, the defendant, said he never saw the man Kirk or Mr. Walton before. He was not in any public-house on the day stated. fle never ordered any goods from Kirk on that or any other day. It was not his signature on the order book.-- By Mr. Carruthers: He remembered the Farnworth Wakes. He was sure that he was not in any hotel during the time. He had hewer seen Mr. Kirk before until that day. He never remembered having aelY transactions before with plaintiffs.—At this point his Honour scrutinised Mr. Brookes' cheque-book and the plaintiff's order-book.—Mr.Carruthers, for the plaintiffs, said the main question was whether that was the signa- ture of the defendant or not. After having examined the signatures he found some similarity between the two.—His Honour said there was only a similarity in the two signatures between the two " b's." He would give judgment for the defendant. It was a serious ease, aid the parties who represented the defendant might well consider if they liked to take further proceedings. He looked upon it as a very serious case. *TRH CLAIM FOR DAMAGES FOR A DOG-BlTE.—This ease was an adjourned one from the previous Court day, in which the plaintiff Michael Flaherty, Corporation labourer, 29 Scott-st., claimed £l5 damages from Joseph Pemberton, 63 Dallam-lane, for the bite of a dog belong- ing to the latter.—Mr. Browne again appeared fer Plain- tiff and Mr. Davies for defendant.—Complainant recalled, deposed t o seeing Mrs. Pemberton:at her house and tell- ing her he was goine off work. She said he could not get anything by going off work as the wound *as only a bit of a scratch.—Mr. Davies: Was the skin broken? —Complainant: Yes air, it was bleeding.—John Galvin, Friar's Green, Warrington, labourer, formerly in the employ of the Corporation as scavenger, said while working in June he was bitten by a dog in Owen- street, which he followed into Pernberton's yard. It was a sheep dog. He showed Mrs. Pemberton his leg and afterwards had it burnt eut.—Joseph 0 wen, foreman of the paihnen, said he remembered seeing Galvin, who complained to him and showed him a dog-bite in the calf ef his leg. It was about midsummer and the bite seemed a bad one.—Robert Cook, 49, Bostock-street,also remembered Galvin being bitten. About three weeks after, witness had his trousers torn by a bite from Pem- berton's dog.—Joseph Pemberton, the defendant, 63, Dallam-lane, said he formerly lived in 0 wen- street, and kept a black and tan collie dog. He destroyed it by an ozder from the Chief Constable. P.C. Evans, saw him drown it. He had not destroyed it because it had bitten other per- sons besides Flaherty. He had never heard of it biting or attacking anyone. He never heard of it biting Galvin.—ln answer to Mr. Davies: He had told Flaherty when he saw him that it was a harmless dog. He had six children living at home.—Mrs. Penaberton, wife of defendant, denied that Galvin was bitten. The dog only jumped up at him. She did not know Cook. Galvin never showed her his leg. Witness offered to pay Flaherty's expenses when be was bitten.—Mr. Davies for the defence contended that he had no case to answer as there was no evidence to show that his client, the owner of the dog, was aware that it had bitten anyone before or that it was savage. There hall been no attempt by Mr. Browne to prove that defend- ant had a knowledge of the dog's vicious- ness. Defendant declared that he never heard of the doe biting or attempting to bite anyone before. —His Honour considered the point and said that the complaint was made to Mrs. Pemberton but there was no evidence to show that she was requested to tell her hnsband of it. The husband said his wife never informed him of it and the evidence of thelplaintiff, showed that there was no request for her to tell her husband. He was afraid he could not Fee that there was any evidence to show that defendant was aware that the dog had bitten anybody before the plaintiff and the case must fail. The evidence was weak with regard to any in- formation being conveyed to the owner of the dog. His Honour regretted very much that the law was net altered in this respect. He saw no hardship in the owner of a dog being called upon to pay damages fcr his dog having bitten someone. He thought it would be better is some public body would take the matter up and endeavour to get the law altered. As it was he must non-suit the plaintiff and allow defendant his costs. A CANCELLED ORDRIL—This was a case brought by Messrs. Walton and Co., of Newton, for £6 51. 11d, for goods alleged to have been ordered and delivered to Mrs. Ada Wilson, of the Sefton Hotel, Cases'-street,Liverpool. Mr. R. Carruthers, Liverpool, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. A. Browne, for the defendant.—Eienry Kirk said he was a traveller for Messrs. Walton and Company.and took the order from Mrs. Wilson for a supply of glass. Ultimately thp order was cancelled. Then be was informed that the goods could be sent on.—By Mr. Browne: He received no wages, but worked on com- mission. He took the order from Mrs. Wilson personally and put it in tne order book. It was in March of the present year that he got the order.—Mr. Walton, plain- tiff, said he was not aware that the order was cancelled. The goods were sent on, and were now lying at the station.—Mr. Browne, for the defendant, said his client kept an hotel in Cases-street, Liverpool, and Kirk went into the house on March last. She was having some alterations done to the house, and he asked her for an order. Seeing that his prices were fair she ordered a quantity of glassware, on the condition that the goods arrived at the end of the week. That was part and parcel of the contract, and was admitted ley Kirk, knowing that the detendant was short of the articles. The fact was that the glass never turned up at the time they were ordered for. The defendant disputed hav- ing given such a big order. The order she gave amounted to about £2 14s.—The defendant, Mrs. Ada Wilson, said she kept the Sefton Hotel, Cases-street, Liverpool. She remembered giving the order on March sth last. She gave evidence as to the quantity ordered. The order did not arrive when Kirk said it would do. The order amounted to about £2 10s. to £2 14s. She had never spoken to Kirk about the order, since. She never can- celed the order, but she considered it was cancelled when Kirk did not send the goods on to time.—His Honour gave judgment for the defendant.

EARLESTOWN A_ND NEWTON.

SUCCESS OF A LOCAL POULTRY FARMER.

Mr. A. Widd, of Earlestown, who has lately been successful on several occasions at various exhibi- tions of poultry, has succeeded this week in carry- ing off the first prize at the Great National Poultry Show held at the Crystal Palace, London, for brown Leghorns. There were 139 entries, covering the whole ot tho United Kingdom, and Mr. Widd has succeeded in bringing this premier prize to Lanca- shire for the first time in the history of the show.

SERIOUS ACCIDENT TO A FOOTBALL PLAYER.

On Saturday afternoon during the progress of the match between v,arlestown and Ashton-in-Maker- field on the Earlestown ground one of the Ashton forwards named Thomas Smith met with a serious injury by colliding with the goal post. He in- tended charging the goalkeeper, but, instead, came into violent contact with the goal post, cutting his head and fallincr to the ground. He was car- ried into the pavilion in an unconscious condition, and after receiving "first aid," was removed home. On enquiry last night we learn that he has been in a serious condition, but is now improving.

Vince, Urquhart, Nurse, Sirett and Appleton. EiR.LBSTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

The 144th quarterly meeting of this Society was held at the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening. Mr. D. Forster presided, and there was a large at- tendance of members. In the report and statement of accounts the Committee said, in placing before the members the statement of accounts for the quarter ending October 13th, 1896, they had plea- sure in again recording a healthy increase in trade. The cash receipts for goods sold were £4,351 Bs. id., being an increase on last quarter of £l5B 13s. 33-11., and compared with the corresponding period of last year showed an increase of £4ll 144. Id. The net profits available for dividend on purchases were £682 9s. Hid. which it was proposed to distribute as follows—to pay a dividend of 24. 101. in the X to members, which would require £638 84. 51 and is. 54. in the £ to non-members, which would ,require 19s. lid., leaving a balance of £33 24. 51. to carry to the dividend reserve fund, and £lO to the contingent fund. Dnring the quarter 27 new mem- bers had been admitted, and 10 struck off, leaving 987 on the books, 686 of whom had brought in checks. The balance sheet wag adopted. The ap- pointment of Mr. Thomas Howarth of Stockport, as secretary and cashier, was confirmed by the meet- ing. Messrs. P. Parr and G. J. Mullins were re- elected to the Management Committee, and Mr, D. Forrester was nominated for the office of President. Authority was given the Committee for the invest- ment of £l,OOO of surplus capital, upon mortgage advances to members in sums not exceeding £3OO. This was all the business, the meeting having been of a most harmonious character.

THE FOG.

WALKING INTO THE CANAL AT RUNCORN.

I narrow escape from drowning occurred near Run- corn on Tuesday morning. A heavy fog' prevailed on the morning in question,when Mr.Thornas West,of Summer- lane, Halt,en. was proceeding to his work at Messrs. Roynolds' Tannery, Astmoor Bridge, Halton, along the Bridgewater Canal, and when nearing the works he walked into the water,so intense was the fog that he was unable to see his way. The mae's cries attracted a num- ber of his work mates,who, after some difficulty get the man out. He was at once attended to and conveyed home. He is in a precarious condition. Mr. West had only just recovered from a very serious illness, and it was the first time he had been out to his work since his illness. The unfortunate man is a cripple, and his fall might have proved fatal had it happened on any other part of the canal, as many people would probably not be about at such an aarly hour-5-30 a.m.

===== 0002601_18961121_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN CANAL TRAFFIC.

The fog at Runcorn on Monday night and Tuesday morning was of such extreme density that the traffic on the river Mersey and the Shi.p. Canal had to be tem- porarily suspended. Later in the day when the mist h d disappeared boats loadel with machißery, china clay, kc., soon made their appearance in the port. By Wed- nesday morning a large number had arrived and an- chored in or about the Runcorn district.

WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.

FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY.

This anniversary in connection with Wycliffe Congre- gational Church and Sunday school was held on Sunday and Tuesday evening. The service in the church on Sunday morning was conducted by the Rev. John Tonge, and in the afternoon an address to scholars and friends was delivered by Mrs. Thomson, returned mite. sionary from Matabeleland. South Africa. After a few introductory remarks by Mr. George Wallington, the superintendent of the school, Mrs. Thomson proceeded to give her address. She described the journey through South Africa to Matabeleland when she first went out, 28 years ego. There were then, she said, no railways in South Africa, and they were obliged to travel in a Cape waggon,which was drawn by oxen. The speaker ex- plained the customs of the people, and alluded to their king—Lobengula. No one was allowed to stand in the presence of the king, and even his own children had to crawl on their hands and knees to him. The Matabele were a brave peeple, and if the king told one of them to go out and obtain the akin of a lion which was devour- ing the cattle he would never came back without it. She (the speaker) was glad to see so many boys and girls present, and she thanked them all for the kind and at- , tentive manner in which they had listened to her. Mrs. I Thomson also spoke in the evening, and gave a general account of the work done by missionaries in South , Africa, referring especially to work in Matabeleland. She said they had only to live amongst the Matabele— who were the warriors—to find that they had hearts like we had, and that they could be touched just 1 as we in England could be. We were too anxious to count the heads of those converted. A few years after new mission stations had been opened out the question was asked," How may converts ?" But we overlooked the great difficulties to be faced. The people were in a state of unpreparedness for the gospel, and years of work were necessary to instruct them. But the missionary's efforts were certainly meeting with suc- cess in South Africa, and if the Leaden Missionary Society had done nothing more than christianize Khama it had done a great deal. A missionary meeting was held in Wycliffe Church on Tuesday evening. The Rev. John Yonge presided, and , referred to the financ;al position of the London Mission- ary Society. He said it was very interesting to know that at the commencement of the second century of the London Missionary Society, the directors had received a larger sum than ever before. The total receipts were £180.900. The home contributions for 1895-6 amounted to £l23,ooo,against £lll,OOO in the previous year. They would see that there was a very substantial increase in I the home contributione, but the greater portion of the increase was due to the larger number of legacies which were made to the Society. With regard to the Centenary fund,the total amount promised was £102,000, and the sum of £BB,OOO had already been received. £42,500 was used to pay off a deficit ; £25,000 had been spent on now mission buildings,and £30,000 had been transferred to the capital account for future years. The contribu- tions to the Society from that church were £293 7s. 4d. for the Centenary fund,and £66 Is. 2d. for the ordinary funds. Referring to the presence of Mr. Bacon of Goety, India, he said many would remember that their brother and friend, Arthur Dignune, laboured in the same dis- trict for some years. Mrs. J. B. Tnomsoer thee addressed the meeting. She referred to the difficulties of missionaries in Matabele- land and to the lives the women lead. She believed that , by the introduction of railways which would penetrate into Central Africa, slavery would be stopped. The Rev. J. R. BACON, from Gooty, India, said it was his pleasure to work in the same mission at Gooty with Mr. Dignum, of Warrington, referred to by the Chair- man. He would put before them the glory of the work of Britain in that vast empire India. He did not believe the English people recognized in any worthy sense the greatness of the glory that rested upon them in having that vast empire within their grasp, responding as it did to the intellectual, the social, and the religious stimulus of our country. India in no sense was to he compared with Africa. From the glistening suns of the Himalay- as, down to the burning plains of Southern India. there was a seething mass of population, industrious, quick to learn, able to reproduce articles in various arts—they were a people who had figured more in history than other nations. Very few of them there would recognise the greatness of the work that was being done by the bandful of English missionaries. The Englishman was always trosted. They did not like to see the red coat of the soldier in Central India because he brought with him vice and love of drink. Though "Tommy Atkins" was a very fine character he might be abetter representative of his great nation. Millions of Hindoos spoke the Eng- lish language. He referred to the great Congress in Madias, at which there were present over 800 people, delegated by men in every part of India to represent every community and discuss the queOions that inter- ested the flindu and the great social difficulties. In In- dia they had a splendid postal service, letters having a halfpenny stamp being carried through the whole of In- dia. There were farthing postcards and inexpensive telegrams. an elaborate parcel post, and numerous other things. Speaking of the progress of Christianity in In- dia, he pointed out that during the past twenty years the number of Christians had trebled. In his own die- trict, when he went in 1878 there were four thousand Christians, now there were 15,000. The greatest num- ber of converts was being made upon the poor classes. In conclusion, he spoke of the great Christian teachers, and said it devolved upon the British people, more than anyone else to supply the need.

A collection was taken n behalf of the funds of the London Missionary Society. A vote of thanks having been passed, the meeting terminated with the Mining of the Doxology.

Ball, S. Lloyd, S. Aspev, H. Pendleourv, J. Mather, J. Mort, G. Garner, R. Kinsley, W. Tweddell, and W. Mitcheson. SUDDEN Dskra.—The Runcorn police have received information of the death of Edward Duncalf, 79 years of age, which took place at the hou.e of his daughter, Heath-road. Weston, on Tuesday mornine%under the fol- lowing circumstances. Deceased had suffered from a weak heart for many rears, and had only just recovered from an attack the previous day. On Tuesday moroi ng the deceased went upstairs to do some repairs to a clock. After about half-an-hour a loud noise—as of someone falling—was heard in another part of the house. His daughter went into the room and found her father lying on the room floor in difficulties. He expired almost im- mediately without uttering a word.

STOCKTO HEATH.

SINGULAR INCIDENT AT A Fox HUNT. 0n Saturday the North Cheshire hounds were out foxhunting in and around the Appleton district. In disturbing the foxes from the covers they disturbed other game. A couple of pheasants in their flight struck against the telegraph wires, and were killed and picked up by the spectators, whilst another flew into the village of Stocktoo Heath. This pheasant in its flight flew through a closed window of an empty house belonging to Mr. Hooley, grocer, of course smashing the glass. Some boys observing this entered the house, when the pheasant, alarmed at the entrance of the boys, forced its way up a chimney, and in that way escaped from the house, but leaving a few feathers behind as an evidence of its struggle through the chimney. When last seen it was making the best of its way back to the cover.

Various decorations and promotions in connection with the Dongola expedition aro announced. Sir Herbert Kitchener becomes a K.C.B. and half a-dezen other officers are made C.B.'s. ATHLETICS.

RIIGIBY FOOTBALL.

At lasr Warrington have olayed an away match and returned with the satisfaction of having staved off de- feat. The fixture last Saturday was with St. Helens at St. Helens, and as the latter team has been doing about as well as Warrington has the reverse it was not ex- pected that the visitors would " break their duck." This anticipation was strengthened by the absence from the Warrington ranks of five players including " Smack " Bate (the captain), and " Fair" Barber. Substitutes were tound in Higginbottom, half-back, and Radford, threequarter. Notwithstanding these adverse conditions the visitors prevented their opponents registering a score and the match ended in a paintloss draw, the first undecided game Warrington have engaged in this sea& 13.

If ever two teams were evenly matched St.Helens and Warrington were on Saturday. All through the game neither side could claim any advantage over the other, and if Warrington had hard lines, St. Helens were sure to be just baulked of a score the next minute. The weather was decidedly against good football and very few passing movements were witnessed. Generally speaking, the game was kick and rush,finding plenty of work for the forwards. Durine. the first few minutes the St. Helens forwards made some very threatening movements by those dribbling tactics which they are famous for. There was scarcely any checking them and the visitors soon saw the utility of such play and replied in similar fashion. Occasionally the game resembled one under the 4ssociation code so long was the ball among the feet of the players.

The smartness of the two Warrington half backs, Houghton and Higginbottom, saved the team from de- feat more than anything else. They generally got the ball and made good use of it. Judicious kicking caused tlae Saints to defend frequently. Cross and O'Hara, the St. Helens couple,were bottled up,and had little opportunity of setting the threequarters going. They made no mis takes,but wore tackled so quickly that they were almost powerless at times. None of the home three- quarters shone brilliantly, though Traynor and Archer were about the best. Doherty was often at fault, while Jacques had little to do. Foulkes played a very steady game as full-back, but the most useful man of the team was Briers, the county for- ward. This player came through the packs at a speed which carried him well up the field before he could be stopped, and he is most useful in heading a dribble. Among the Warrington forwards, Taylor and Thornton were the best. Footwork suited them on Saturday, and they were as prominent as Briers was on the other side. Lawless and Moores were the pick of the threequarter line, and both played a dashing game. Moores was par- ticularly dexterous in snapping up passes meant for Traynor, and on three occasions he got away speedily by it. His following up and kicking were also very useful. Lawless put in several lengthy runs and once more saved a certain try right on the line. For the last three or four matches he has prevented some wide-awake opponent improving his scoring average. Cross (Warrington) played moderately well, while Radford was of little use. Eleesem came out strongly on two or three occasions by the plucky eaves he made. More than once the game depended on his safety.

Now that they have played away from horn. and secured a point Warrington should redouble their efforts and initiate another!series of victories. They have it in them, with ordinary luck.

To-day's match has been eagerly looked forward to for the last three weeks. Widnes made a mess of Warring- ton on their own ground and the wirepullers are dying to wipe out the stigma at Wilderspool this afternoon. Since the first meeting of the clubs this season, Widnes have upheld their reputation for winning and losing alternately. If they are going to be consistent they should certainly lose here for they obtained two points out of Morecambe a week ago. The result of a game between these two teams is always an uncertainty, so keen is local rivalry.

By the appended league table of results it will be seen that Warrington are not maintaining their improved form, but gradually sinking towards the bottom again. Only four clubs are now below them, and may any week obtain the lead. Just three points separate War- rington from the tail end.

Oldham have at last outrun Stockport, and reached the top of the list. With a record of nine games played and nine w,n they have ineieed a record to be proud of. Their scoring powers are as great as ever, for they have no less than 115 points for and 12 against.

Many footballers think that Stockport have got to come down in the world. They have been fortunate se far in playing a majority of hotne matches, and in some of these luck has turned the scale.

Wretched weather prevailed on Sxturdav at Manchs- ter, when the engagement between Runcorn and Brough- ton Rangers wag decided. It is hoped that the weather at home this week end will be of a somewhat milder character, as the encounter between the cupholders,and their county brothers, is a very important one. Runcorn are six points behind the Stockport fifteen.

The game last week ended in the Runeornians being the victors after a hard battle. When the half-time message viz., Broughton Rangers a try, Runcorn nil, landed in Runcorn, it was thought by many that. the cuphelders would have to knuckle down to such a corn- bination. as shown by the Manchester team in previous matches. This was not so,as the visitors:after changing ends put some power in their play,and appeared to be as fresh as ever. J. Faulkner did good work by his clever dribbling. A magnificent bout of passing so successfully carried to the homesters goal line was performed in grand style hut with no rpsult by G. Robinson, J. Faulkner, Richardson,and Butterworth. The homestere had now a very anxious time of it,Run- corn being in closo proximity to their goal line almost the whole of the rest of the gams. Houghton had not much to perform, but what little he had he did splen- didly. J. Faulkner then earned himself fame by dropp- ing a goal and soon after adding a try to the Runcorn score. Like Butterworth in a previous match he was the hero of the day.

The homesters', weight was met by science and the result may be looked upon as one more trumph of mind over matter.

INQUESTS AT WARRINGTON.-SUICIDE OF AN OLD MAN.

On Monday at the Parade Room Warrington Mr. S. Brighouse (coroner) conducted an inquest on the body of Richard Wilcock, 12. Atherton-street Warrington, aged 80 years, who committed suicide by cutting his throat on the 9th inst.—John Wilcock said the deeeased was his lather, and lived with them. On the morning of the 9th inst., de- ceased asked him what time it was. He (witness) did not see him and knew nothing of what had happened until he came to his breakfast.—Sarah Ann Wilcock said the deceased was her father-in- law. On the morning of the 9th inst., about 7.30, as she was coming out of the pantry she saw de- ceased,who was standing near the fire. She could see that his throat was bleeding, and she ran out as she was afraid. She met a constable and told him what had happened.—P. C. Lewis stated that he went with the last witness to the house. He there saw deceased, who was sitting near the fire. Blood was flowing from his throat and mouth. Witness rendered first aid, and asked deceased what he had done it with and he replied that he had done it with a razor which was upstairs. Witness found the two razors and a knife (pro- duced), which were covered with blood.—Nurse IClarke stated that deceased was admitted to the Infirmary between 7.30 and eight o'clock. He was suffering from a lacerated throat. He died on Friday. The jury returned a verdict of" Suicide whilst of unsound mind.'—The coroner then held an inquiry into the death of Lucy Tarwood, who resided at Garibaldi-street. and who sustained fatal scalds on the 12th inst ,—Mary Alice Yarwood said she resided at 8, Garibaldi-street. The deceased was her daughter, andwas aged ei2ht months. The accident took place on Thursday, 12th inst. On the above date she left a pan, which contained turnips, on the bar of the firegrate. rhere was a guard in front of the fire, and when she returned she found the pan had been upset, by what means she did not know, and the child was scalded. She died at half- past eleven on Saturday morning from the effects of the scalds. A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned,

The toad captures insects by darting out its tongue so rapidly that the eye cannot follow the motion. The tip is covered with a glutinous secretion, to which a fly or other mall insect adheres.

The Rome correspondent of the " Daily News" says : —ln aristocratic circles here for the last few days there has been much talk about the elopement of a primes.. Only to-night (Monday), however, do the papers openly state that Princess Elvira, daughter of Don Carlos, has eloped with Count Folchi, an artist, a married man, and a member of the Clerical Society. The iPrincess fled from the palace cf Princess Massimo here, where she has been visiting. She tonk with her all her jewels,aniount- ing to an immense sum in value.
